Trump (right) repeated his claim that Ukraine was targeting Russian refineries to hurt Republicans ahead of the US midterm election [FILE: September 22, 2026] Image: Julia Demaree Nikhinson/AP Photo/picture alliance Advertisement
US President Donald Trump &#160;on Sunday defended his decision to waive some sanctions on Russia, accusing Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy of not wanting to end the war.
"My decision on Russia is a very simple one. I am in the process of negotiating Peace in the war between Russia and Ukraine, and don't want to do anything that will drive, foolishly, Russia away from the bargaining table," Trump wrote on Truth Social.
"Zelenskyy doesn't care, and frankly, with all of the money that Europe is famously giving him, and that Crooked Joe Biden gave him in the past, he doesn't want to end this war, and probably never did," he added.
Trump signed an executive order late Friday waiving broad new sanctions mandated by legislation named for the late US Senator&#160;Lindsey Graham, a staunch supporter of Ukraine's defense.
He has also temporarily waived sanctions on Russian diesel, allowing supplies to flow into global markets in a bid to lower fuel prices ahead of US midterm&#160;elections on November&#160;3.
Ukraine strikes Putin's hometown, as Trump eases pressure on Russia

Trump links Russia sanctions waiver to fuel prices ahead of midterms
Opinion polls suggest that Republicans could face significant losses in the election, as voters voice growing frustration over fuel prices that have surged since the Iran war began in late February.&#160;
Most economists believe the suspension of congressionally approved sanctions on Russia and the diesel deal with the Kremlin will do little to lower US fuel prices.
Trump also repeated his claim that Ukraine was targeting Russian refineries to hurt Republicans ahead of the midterm election.
The move drew a sharp response from Kyiv and its allies, further straining relations between Trump and Zelenskyy.
Meanwhile, on Sunday, the Wall Street Journal reported that Russian President Vladimir Putin had proposed a meeting with Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping the following month to discuss ending the war in Ukraine.
According to the report, which cited people familiar with the discussions, Zelenskyy would not be included in the proposed talks, which&#160;would seek to end the war&#160;on terms favorable to Moscow.

Zelenskyy warns Ukraine cannot prevail if US backs Russia
Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy warned Sunday that Ukraine would lose its defensive battle against Russia if the United States switched sides and supported Moscow.
"If the United States will take the side of Russia, of course we will not prevail," Zelenskyy told NBC's 'Meet the Press'&#160;calling a withdrawal of US support "a historic mistake and big tragedy."
He said Washington had resumed sharing intelligence after it was "blocked for some period of time," adding that Ukraine had missed critical intelligence on ballistic missile attacks for three days.
Zelenskyy also criticized US restrictions affecting Ukraine's war effort, saying, "we are strategic partners," but "we cannot applaud when someone turns against Ukraine."
Meanwhile, US Secretary of State Marco Rubio&#160;defended Trump's suspension of new sanctions on Russia, saying Trump wanted to assess their impact on the global and US economies.

Volodymyr Zelenskyy spoke to US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner as Russia and Ukraine attacked each other's energy infrastructure, leaving thousands without power in Ukraine and setting an oil refinery ablaze near Saint Petersburg.
After the talks, Zelenskyy said "a lot depends" on the US position, adding that "this war must be brought to an end." Ukraine was ready to work "constructively" and "honestly,"&#160;he said, calling for the same approach from its European and US partners.
Zelenskyy said he also discussed deliveries of US Patriot air defense systems as Kyiv struggles to counter Russian missiles.
A US-led push to end the war has yet to yield a breakthrough, with Trump expressing frustration at both Zelenskyy and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Cyril Widdershoven is a senior maritime, energy, and geopolitical analyst and Senior Advisor at Blue Water Strategy, specialising in the strategic intersection of shipping, ports,&#8230;
Hormuz Is Turning Shipping’s Fuel Bill Into a Supply Crisis
The Hormuz crisis is tightening global bunker fuel supplies, with disrupted Middle Eastern and Russian exports driving shortages and longer delivery times.
Soaring marine fuel prices are squeezing shipowners, increasing voyage costs, working capital requirements, and pressure on profit margins.
Global shipping faces growing disruption, as unreliable refueling, limited inventories, and tighter credit threaten trade routes and vessel schedules.
The Hormuz crisis is not only hitting crude oil and gas, but also container shipping. It is also tightening its grip on global shipping through the fuel tanks of vessels carrying world trade. Disrupted exports, constrained refinery supplies, and increasingly unreliable deliveries are clearly at present pushing the bunker market into a dangerous position. Fuel remains available internationally, but securing the right grade at the right port within a commercially workable window is becoming harder. The consequences will be severe, especially for shipowners, as it extends beyond expensive bunkers. Delayed deliveries and uncertain replenishment are not only threatening voyage schedules but also working capital and the profitability of entire trading routes.
This pressure is measurable. On October 16, Vitol estimated that high-sulfur fuel oil flows through Hormuz, including Iranian supplies, remained at least 800,000 tons per month below February levels. At the same time, the successful attacks by Ukraine on Russian refineries have removed an additional estimated million tons monthly. Meanwhile, North Asian refiners were drawing more than a million tons of HSFO for November processing. Global shipping is therefore competing for a constrained product pool. This is a growing reality, even though low-sulfur supplies have received some support from increased exports from China and Nigeria.
Related: German Power Supply Startups Exit Market as Wholesale Prices Soar
These pressures are now converging on a market that depends as much on reliable distribution as on aggregate production. A ton of fuel awaiting export, committed to another customer or stranded behind a disrupted shipping route cannot satisfy an immediate bunker requirement. The market still doesn't seem to recognize this fully. Refinery output, export capacity, blending components, storage and barge availability must all align before a vessel can refuel. Hormuz has weakened several of those links at once, allowing disruption in one location to ripple through the wider supply chain. The spreading of conflict in the Middle East, especially in and around the Bab El Mandeb and Saudi Arabia, is only increasing the impact.
Fujairah illustrates both the market’s adaptability and its continuing vulnerability. Reuters reported on October 1 that third-quarter fuel oil imports had recovered to 2.6 million tons from 845,000 tons in the second quarter. Nevertheless, September imports remained approximately half February’s level. Yes, supplies were returning, but through a system still operating well below earlier import volumes. A partial recovery in cargo movements cannot establish dependable bunker availability when replenishment remains exposed to renewed interruption. The current security situation in and around Hormuz and the Gulf of Oman doesn’t bode well.
The latest delivery assessments show how uneven that recovery remains. ENGINE’s October 13 outlook put Singapore’s recommended very-low-sulfur fuel oil lead times at 11–18 days, compared with 10–15 days a week earlier. HSFO required 11–17 days. Fujairah’s VLSFO availability had improved, while HSFO and low-sulfur marine gasoil remained limited. Key Omani ports offered a more comfortable marine gasoil position, with one supplier quoting one to two days. The emerging crisis is geographically fragmented, with pressure varying by fuel grade and delivery location.
That fragmentation complicates procurement, without making the commercial damage less serious. A ship requiring fuel within three days cannot necessarily absorb a two-week booking window. Physical shortages become commercially disruptive well before a port exhausts its stocks. The critical threshold will be crossed when available supply can no longer accommodate the schedules on which shipping contracts depend.
Inventory figures can consequently offer misleading reassurance. ENGINE reported that Fujairah’s heavy distillate and residual stocks averaged 4.72 million barrels in early October, approximately 5% above September’s average. At the same time, local bunker availability remains severely constrained. Those inventories do not all represent uncommitted, immediately deliverable marine fuel. Product specifications, ownership, and export obligations determine how much can actually reach a vessel. More barrels in storage can coexist with fewer practical purchasing options for an owner facing a departure deadline.
Here, the market and market participants play a pivotal role. The economics of holding inventory add another vulnerability. S&P Global reported in September that strong backwardation, with prompt fuel priced above later deliveries, was discouraging blenders from maintaining stocks. Blending closer to delivery reduced their exposure to falling prices. However, it also increases sensitivity to delayed or unsuitable components. Across a stressed market, individually rational decisions can remove the collective buffer needed to absorb disruption. We are now looking at a situation in which a replenishment delay that would previously have been manageable can then translate directly into a failed delivery.
At the same time, shippers are looking at financial constraints too. Higher prices are already imposing a substantial financing burden. General Index assessed September VLSFO averages at $872 per ton in Singapore and $939 in Fujairah, up 6% and 15.9%, respectively, from August. An additional $100 per ton increases the cost of a 2,000-ton purchase by $200,000, before accounting for waiting time or diversion expenses. Shippers will calculate all of this; if it repeats across a fleet, such increases demand considerably more cash to maintain existing operations. It seems the media still doesn’t understand that even strong headline freight earnings don't automatically translate into stronger margins.
The next constraint could emerge through credit. When fuel prices rise, an unchanged financing facility buys fewer tons. Suppliers need additional funding to maintain throughput, while owners need more liquidity to cover each voyage. If lenders or counterparties tighten terms, physical availability may be accompanied by financial exclusion: fuel can be purchased, but only by buyers able to provide sufficient cash or security. Smaller operators would be particularly vulnerable to this combination of expensive deliveries and reduced purchasing flexibility.
Owners are already adjusting. Reuters reported on October 16 that bunker purchases were becoming smaller and more frequent, with shorter contractual commitments and greater use of alternative locations, including Port Louis, Colombo, and Salalah. This not only helps operators manage immediate exposure but also shifts demand toward ports that must secure additional replenishment and delivery capacity. Geographic diversification improves options; its effectiveness still depends on the fuel and infrastructure available behind each alternative call.
Over the coming weeks, regional shortages will recur, premiums will remain volatile, and booking requirements will lengthen. A broader crisis will occur if additional export losses coincide with refinery outages and reduced tanker availability.
Shipping does not need every bunker hub to run dry for the damage to become systemic. Schedules will be disrupted if persistent uncertainty over delivery remains in place. Hormuz is steadily turning dependable refueling into a more expensive and conditional service. Customers far beyond the Gulf will be feeling the consequences.

### 5. ★★★☆☆ EU tariffs aimed at China hitting Ukraine, steel boss says

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business / GN China
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-18T23:28:45+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The boss of one of Ukraine's biggest steel companies says the country has been "bundled together" in measures aimed at curbing cheap imports.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

By Michael Race ,Â  Business reporter Â  and Â  Simon Jack ,Â  Business editor
The boss of one of Ukraine&#x27;s biggest steel companies has called on the European Union to drop import restrictions on products made from the country, arguing it has been "bundled together" in measures aimed a curbing cheap imports from the likes of China.
Yuriy Ryzhenkov, chief executive of Metinvest, told the BBC the recently-imposed tariffs on imported steel to the EU were "unfair" and had been put in place "without any consideration for the situation Ukraine is in".
He said he wished to see "at least a carve out" for Ukraine to access the EU market.
A spokesperson for the European Commission said the EU "took into account the difficult situation" of Ukraine when deciding its steel policy.
The trading bloc has recently imposed restrictions on steel imports in an effort to protect steel manufacturers of member nations.
It has capped the amount of steel which can enter the EU tax free at 18.3 million tonnes per year - a 47% reduction from 2024 levels.
There are some country-specific quotas in the policy, but the overall aim is to cut the amount of steel that can be imported due to member nations struggling to compete with cheap imports from the likes of China and Turkey.
Despite a free-trade agreement (FTA) being in place between the EU and Ukraine, the FTA does not stop the EU from introducing separate trade protection measures.
"Ukraine has been bundled together into those measures without any consideration for the situation Ukraine is in," Ryzhenkov said, pointing to the war with Russia.
"Access to the European market is crucial for survival," he added. "The restrictive measures that have been put in place by the authorities in the European Union, they don&#x27;t help.
"I think it&#x27;s unfair that Ukraine has been treated in the same way as all of the other importers into the European Union."
The Ukraine steel industry estimates steel exports to the EU could ultimately fall by about 50â60%.
The EU said Ukraine&#x27;s specific steel quota guaranteed exports of the metal, but acknowledged this was at a "level lower than in the past years".
"Despite the war effort, Ukraine remains an important exporter of steel to the EU," a spokesperson said, adding it had "offered compensations" to its largest suppliers, which included Ukraine.
Image caption, Yuriy Ryzhenkov, chief executive of Metinvest, says EU steel quotas are "unfair" on Ukraine
Steel products are a foundation in Ukraine&#x27;s economy. Before the start of Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion in 2022, metallurgy and mining accounted for roughly 10% of Ukraine&#x27;s national income and a third of its exports. The EU is its top customer.
But the industry has suffered from the continued Russian bombardment.
All of Ukraine&#x27;s steel mills have been bombed at points since the conflict began in 2022, but Ryzhenkov said strikes had "intensified significantly recently".
At one point in September, there was "no single tonne of steel produced" in Ukraine for the first time in about 150 years, he added.
Almost 800 Metinvest workers have been killed since the war began.
Ryzhenkov said the company had "improved the covered areas" at its mills where workers can shelter during air raids.
"The only thing we can do is to design procedures to keep people safe and just obey those procedures and try to minimise the risks."
"Unfortunately, you cannot cover a whole steel mill to avoid it being damaged."
Ryzhenkov said he expected the coming winter to be "one of the hardest in our history".
"We are in a dire situation when we don&#x27;t have logistics to access any other market apart from the EU and cutting our available market by 60%, that doesn&#x27;t help for the industry to survive."

</details>

### 6. ★★★☆☆ Qatar warns global energy shockwaves from Iran war have yet to peak

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T09:18:24+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Deputy PM calls for dialogue with Tehran, highlights US defence ties and rejects Strait of Hormuz transit fees.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Qatar warns global energy shockwaves from Iran war have yet to peak on social media
Qatar's Deputy Prime MInister Sheikh Saoud bin Abdulrahman Al Thani, speaking at the Global Security Forum in Doha on Monday, October 19, 2026 [Screen grab/Global Security Forum]
Doha, Qatar — Qatar’s deputy prime minister has warned that global energy shockwaves from the US-Israel war on Iran have yet to reach their peak, raising concerns about further disruption to supplies, international trade and economic growth.
Speaking at the Global Security Forum in Doha on Monday, Sheikh Saoud bin Abdulrahman Al Thani, who also serves as Minister of State for Defence Affairs, identified several threats facing Qatar and the wider Gulf, including risks to sovereignty and disruptions to energy supplies.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Iran’s president condemns environmental impact of war in Middle East
list 2 of 3 How Iran messaging highlights cost of war ahead of US midterm elections
list 3 of 3 US and Iran both claim control of Strait of Hormuz amid attacks on vessels
“We haven’t reached the peak with respect to energy,” he said, warning of further repercussions “in the coming days and months”.
His remarks come amid continued instability in the Strait of Hormuz – a vital waterway for global oil and liquefied natural gas (LNG) shipments – following the US-Israeli attacks on Iran on February 28, and Tehran’s retaliatory strikes across the region.
Iranian strikes on Qatar’s Ras Laffan industrial complex in March damaged facilities, with repairs expected to take several years.
“We were surprised,” Al Thani said when asked about the attack, adding that there had since been “a lot of discussion with Iran”.
He said Qatar had drawn lessons from the strikes, “to make sure that all of our assets will be protected from any unexpected attack”.
Al Thani also warned against imposing tolls on ships passing through the Strait of Hormuz.
“Transit fees should not be applied,” he said, adding that uncertainty over the waterway would ultimately affect consumers worldwide.
Despite the attacks, Al Thani said Qatar continued to maintain contact with Tehran.
“We never stop our communication,” he said, adding that the two countries remained in touch at different levels despite their disagreements.
He described Qatar’s relationship with Iran before the attacks as one of trust, pointing to their shared offshore gasfield and longstanding economic interests.
Al Thani expressed hope that mediation efforts involving Qatar, Pakistan and Oman would help produce an agreement between the United States, Iran and other parties, including Israel.
He said a sustainable settlement would require broader regional dialogue involving Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC) countries, Pakistan and Turkiye, in consultation with international allies.
Al Thani also suggested developing economic ties with Iran to prevent future conflicts, citing cooperation between France and Germany following decades of hostilities.
“Iran is our neighbour. We need to find ways to live with them,” he said.
Al Thani also highlighted Qatar’s longstanding defence partnership with Washington, saying that “more than 90 percent” of attacks had been prevented through shared expertise and military capabilities developed over the past three to four decades.
He said Qatar was continuing to develop Al Udeid airbase, which hosts US forces and military personnel from other partner countries.
The base was itself targeted by Iran during the conflict, with Qatar reporting that it had shot down two Iranian warplanes during an attack in early March.
Al Thani also announced plans for a new regional defence partnership, saying Qatar was prioritising several aspects including air defence, counter-drone measures and cybersecurity.
“I’m happy to announce today also that soon we’re going to announce a new partnership,” he said, without naming the regional partners involved.
The deputy prime minister stressed that Qatar’s mediation efforts did not diminish its commitment to defending its territory.
“Mediation doesn’t mean that we should not be ready for any defensive action from our side,” he said.

</details>

The report comes amid mounting ten

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Putin reportedly suggests summit with Trump, Xi about Ukraine
The Wall Street Journal reported Vladimir Putin had reportedly suggested a summit between himself, U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping for a deal to end the Ukraine war.
The summit is not confirmed, but if it takes place will not involve Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy.
The report comes amid mounting tensions between Ukraine and the U.S., its largest military backer.
Russian President Vladimir Putin has reportedly floated a three-way summit between himself, U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping to negotiate an end to the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.
The meeting was mentioned in a phone call to Trump on Oct. 9, the Wall Street Journal reported on Monday, citing a former Russian intelligence officer briefed on the matter. Such a summit may take place at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in November in the Chinese city of Shenzhen, according to the report.
The call also produced a deal for Moscow to sell diesel on the global market.
U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said early Monday that "there&#x27;s been talks of a meeting. I don&#x27;t know whether it&#x27;s been finalized, but the president has been clear," he said in comments reported by Reuters.
"The president is open to anything that he can do to help bring a war to an end, if it&#x27;s possible, and we&#x27;ll continue to look for opportunities to do that," Rubio told reporters during a visit to Uzbekistan.
It comes amid mounting tensions between Ukraine and the U.S., its largest military backer. Over the past week, a series of moves by the Trump administration has raised fresh questions about Washington&#x27;s reliability as Kyiv&#x27;s most important military partner. It caused alarm among European allies and prompted criticism from fellow Republicans.
U.S. President Donald Trump (R) greets Russian President Vladimir Putin as he arrives at Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson on August 15, 2025 in Anchorage, Alaska.
Over the weekend, Trump defended his decision to temporarily lift sanctions on Russian diesel exports, as well as an executive order on Oct. 18 to waive new Russia sanctions from a bill introduced by the late Senator Lindsey Graham.
Trump and Zelenskyy have traded criticism over how to end the war in Ukraine, with Trump accusing the Ukrainian president of not wanting peace , while Zelenskyy has argued that Washington&#x27;s easing of sanctions on Russia risks prolonging the conflict. In a post on X, Zelenskyy said that ″the decision to undo all of this, to block all of it, seems wrong to me."
The tensions come as Russia launched another wave of overnight attacks on Ukraine, firing 16 ballistic missiles at Kyiv and the surrounding region and more than 200 drones across the country, according to Zelenskyy.
In a post on X on Monday, Zelenskyy said the strikes damaged more than 10 energy infrastructure facilities, leaving at least 550,000 households without power. He also said Ukraine had once again received no advance warning from its partners about the missile attacks. Casualties were reported, while a medical clinic, school, metro station and residential buildings were also damaged.
Ukraine&#x27;s First Deputy Minister for Recovery, Infrastructure and Transport Serhiy Derkach told CNBC on Monday that Kyiv still hoped to retain Washington&#x27;s support despite the growing tensions.
Asked whether the Trump administration was abandoning Ukraine and siding with Russia, Derkach said he did not believe the U.S. was abandoning Kyiv, but stressed that Ukraine hoped Washington would continue helping it or at least "stay in the middle."
"That&#x27;s most important for us actually, not to lose the support of the U.S.," he said.
Russia has also intensified attacks on Ukraine&#x27;s transport infrastructure, including road border crossings, railways and shipping routes, Derkach said.
It comes after Trump told Ukraine to stop striking Russian energy infrastructure, claiming that it was driving up global diesel prices.
Despite an "energy ceasefire" announced by Trump on Oct. 11, Russia continued striking Ukrainian infrastructure. Kyiv paused its attacks for three days before resuming them last Thursday, citing continued Russian strikes.
"America should see that Russia is not taking any reciprocal steps in response to sanctions relief or agreements. On the contrary, they take every signal of an opportunity to make money as proof that their bet on war is paying off and they can keep fighting," the Ukrainian president wrote on Sunday.
On Sunday, he also told Bloomberg that "steps have been taken to convince Ukraine to capitulate."
"I am not saying that this is the goal our partners are pursuing. I don&#x27;t want to think that our American partners believe the war" against Russia "could be ended sooner at Ukraine&#x27;s expense. But that&#x27;s how it looks."
Over the weekend, both Kyiv and Moscow traded strikes, with Ukraine launching a strike of over 1,000 drones into Russia, setting fires at an oil export terminal and refinery in the city of St. Petersburg, while Moscow pounded Kyiv with ballistic missiles.
Last week, Kyiv also accused Washington of ordering restrictions on its Starshield networks, the military-grade version of SpaceX&#x27;s Starlink. Ukraine relies on this connectivity to track and intercept incoming threats.
SpaceX CEO Elon Musk pinned responsibility for any restrictions on the U.S. government and Trump, writing on X that "Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the U.S. government."
Trump denied on Friday that the White House had ordered restrictions on Starlink satellite internet services provided to Ukraine&#x27;s armed forces, after Reuters reported on Thursday that the call to restrict Starlink in Ukraine came from someone inside the White House, citing two U.S. officials.

The Telegraph reported on Friday that Musk "approached Donald Trump with the idea of slowing down Starlink in Ukraine," and both had a hand in imposing the restrictions.
Additionally, Zelenskyy told NBC&#x27;s Meet the Press on Sunday that intelligence sharing between the two countries had been interrupted, but later "de-blocked."
Rubio on Monday denied that Washington had suspended intelligence sharing with Ukraine, contradicting Zelenskyy&#x27;s claim that the flow of information had been briefly interrupted, Reuters reported.
Speaking to reporters in Uzbekistan, Rubio insisted there had been no pause and said Zelenskyy should instead focus on ending the war, marking another public disagreement between Washington and Kyiv amid deteriorating relations.

### 8. ★★★☆☆ US gave us no warning of Russian strikes on Kyiv power plants, Zelenskyy says - The Guardian

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / Guardian World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T09:06:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US gave us no warning of Russian strikes on Kyiv power plants, Zelenskyy says&nbsp;&nbsp;The Guardian

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Workers repair railway tracks at a metro station hit by an overnight Russian missile strike on Kyiv. Photograph: Valentyn Ogirenko/Reuters
Workers repair railway tracks at a metro station hit by an overnight Russian missile strike on Kyiv. Photograph: Valentyn Ogirenko/Reuters
US gave us no warning of Russian strikes on Kyiv power plants, Zelenskyy says
Ukrainian president accuses Washington of ‘denial of reality’ in ongoing row with Trump over apparent support for Moscow
Ukraine did not receive a warning from the US about an impending Russian missile attack on Sunday evening, Volodymyr Zelenskyy has said, reviving concerns that Washington is withholding intelligence in order to bully Kyiv into capitulating to Moscow.
Yuri Ihnat, a spokesperson for the Ukrainian Air Force, said on television on Monday morning that Ukraine had not received a warning before a Russian missile barrage hit power plants in Kyiv at around 11pm. Earlier, a Ukrainian government official had told the Guardian the US had not warned the capital about the impending missile strike.
Ukraine did receive a warning before a successive strike, at 3am, Ihnat added.
“Once again, Russia launched a ballistic missile attack in multiple waves throughout the night, and once again, there was no warning from our partners,” Zelenskyy wrote in a social media post on Monday morning.
“When the supply of missiles for Ukrainian air defence is blocked, and Russia receives sanctions relief or delays, it is, unfortunately, a denial of reality on the part of our partners,” the president added.
On Friday, Donald Trump waived a sweeping package of sanctions on Russia , telling Congress the pause on the legislation, named after the late senator Lindsey Graham and passed by both chambers, was in the national interest. Ukraine has also complained of slowed deliveries of PAC-3 air defence missiles from the US.
The US president wrote on Truth Social on Sunday: “My decision on Russia is a very simple one. I am in the process of negotiating peace in the war between Russia and Ukraine, and don’t want to do anything that will drive, foolishly, Russia away from the bargaining table.
“Zelenskyy doesn’t care, and frankly, with all of the money that Europe is famously giving him … he doesn’t want to end this war , and probably never did.”
For years, the US had warned Ukraine before Russia launches ballistic missiles, giving authorities several minutes to sound alarms and allow civilians to reach shelters before the strikes hit. However, Ukraine has said it was not warned on at least three days in the past week before missiles were launched, amid a public quarrel between Zelenskyy and Trump.
Speaking in the early hours of Monday on a trip to Samarkand, Uzbekistan, the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, said: “If it happened last night, I don’t know exactly. I know a few nights ago there was a software problem involving their early warning system.”
US officials have not previously mentioned a software glitch in the early warning system. In at least one case, Ukrainian officials had been told that bad weather over southern Russia had obscured preparations for the missile launch. Rubio said the military’s European Command was looking into the strikes.
The US has maintained plausible deniability as services and programmes provided to support Ukraine have suddenly begun to fail. The Trump administration has denied that it throttled services in Ukraine for Starlink , the satellite internet service run by Elon Musk that is used both on offensive drones and anti-air weapons by Ukraine.
Ukraine has also said US weapons delivered through the PURL scheme, where Europe purchases US weapons then delivers them to Ukraine, have been delayed. The Trump administration reportedly threatened Kyiv with a slowdown in crucial weapons deliveries unless it stopped striking Russian oil refineries.

Follow updates on the war in the Middle East for Monday, October 19, here . See earlier developments below.
Iran's foreign minister said Sunday the Strait of Hormuz would reopen within days if the U.S. complies with the conditions set out in the seven-day ceasefire proposal presented in September, which called for an end to the American naval blockade, lifting sanctions on Iranian oil and a ceasefire on all fronts.
The U.K. Maritime Trade Operations monitoring agency said at least 10 vessels have been attacked in or near the Strait of Hormuz since last Sunday, a weekly high since war broke out in February, and 23 attacks were recorded since the start of the month, almost double the average.
Yemen's foreign minister blasted Iran's offer to mediate the war between the internationally recognized government and the Iran-backed Houthi militant group. "When Iran's foreign minister and his officials offer 'mediation' in Yemen, they are offering to mediate a war ignited by the coup of the Houthi terrorist militia with their regime's backing," she said.
Saudi-led military coalition and Houthis trade strikes amid intensifying conflict
Conflict raged between the Houthis and the Saudi-led military coalition fighting the Iran-backed rebel group on Sunday, as both sides traded strikes.
In Riyadh, where intensifying violence has disrupted life for residents, the landmark Kingdom Centre skyscraper ordered "mandatory" remote working for office tenants until further notice. Although a reason for the order wasn't provided, it followed a security alert and two drone interceptions around the capital city, in addition to a number of attacks by Houthis on Saudi targets in recent weeks.
Meanwhile, the Saudi-led coalition, which backs the internationally-recognized government in war-wracked Yemen, has launched repeated air raids on regions of the country that Houthis control. One alleged Saudi airstrike in northern Yemen killed at least nine people and wounded 16 others on Sunday, the Houthis' Saba news agency reported.
The coalition said Sunday that two pilots were killed when their helicopter crashed in Najran, near the Yemen border, blaming a "technical malfunction" for the incident. The Houthis said they "downed an Apache helicopter belonging to the Saudi enemy" in the same area.

Netanyahu says partnership with Trump will help "eliminate Iranian threat"
Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu praised President Trump in multiple social media posts on Sunday, saying his relationship with the U.S. president will help "eliminate the Iranian threat." His comments came after Mr. Trump endorsed Netanyahu as the Israeli leader seeks reelection in a tight race.
In a video released by his Likud party, Netanyahu called on voters to heed Mr. Trump's words, assuring them "we have crushed Iran" and adding that their partnership "will continue in order to eliminate the Iranian threat over the next two years, or even sooner."

Iraq says Iran war has deprived it of $60 billion in oil export revenue
Iraq has lost around $60 billion during the U.S.-Iran war, Foreign Minister Fuad Hussein said Thursday, as the fighting has blocked or severely impeded a waterway crucial to its oil exports.
OPEC member Iraq was producing about 4 million barrels of oil per day (bpd) before the conflict, and exporting about 3.5 million bpd &ndash; mostly carried by tankers out of the Persian Gulf via the Strait of Hormuz.
Iran's ongoing threat to vessels that fail to seek its permission to transit the strait has forced Iraq, which lacks sufficient storage capacity, to halt most production and fall back on limited exports via Syria and Turkey, by truck and pipeline respectively. Syria is set to provide an overland oil route to bypass the strait , the head of Syrian Petroleum Co. said last week.
"Iraq has suffered losses estimated at around $60 billion during the recent war between the United States and the Islamic Republic of Iran," Hussein told his Italian counterpart in Rome, according to a statement from the Iraqi foreign ministry.
After a heavy drop in production, Iraqi oil exports, which provide about 90% of the country's revenues, recovered modestly last month, reaching 2.6 million barrels per day between Sept. 1 and 21, according to the oil ministry. But at the same time, Iraq's foreign currency reserves have shrunk by $20 billion and consumer prices have risen sharply, plunging it into economic crisis.

Norwegian teen sentenced in U.K. to 20 years in prison for plotting murder for Iran-linked gang
A Norwegian teenager convicted of traveling to Britain to carry out a contract killing on behalf of an international crime gang linked to Iran was sentenced Friday to 20 years in prison.
Johannes Kongsnes Natland, 19, was found guilty in July of conspiracy to murder for agreeing to shoot someone unknown to him for $28,000 last year. He was arrested at a hotel in Huddersfield, in northern England, with two guns and ammunition two days after arriving in the U.K.
In bodycam video released by police from the early hours of March 19, 2025, Natland emerges topless from his room and mimes a shooting gesture at a firearms officer before being slammed against a wall.
It remains unknown who his intended target would have been.
Natland, who was working part time at a pizza restaurant at the time of his arrest, was recruited by a Swedish organized crime group known as the Foxtrot Network while he was playing a game online. The group has links to the Iranian government, according to London's counterterrorism police unit.
Natland was hired by a 17-year-old known as "Generalen." A court in Norway last year convicted the teenager of attempting to "contribute to murder" and sentenced him to 14 years in custody. A 16-year-old is awaiting trial in Norway for his role in the conspiracy.

Iran president says conflict in Mideast amounts to "ecocide"
Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian on Friday urged countries to condemn what he called "ecocide" in the Middle East caused by "wars of aggression" against his country.
Pezeshkian said the conflicts in Gaza and the wider Middle East had taken a significant toll on nature that extended beyond national borders and required "international solutions."
"What has occurred in our region as a result of wars of aggression is ecocide, or environmental genocide, and it must be condemned," Pezeshkian said at a nature summit in Armenia that was attended by United Nations Secretary-General Antonio Guterres.
Pezeshkian decried an "atmosphere of distrust" between Iran and the U.S. in a recent interview with CBS News .
"As long as they don't cease doing what they're imposing on us every day, it's difficult to trust them," he said. "Every day, they increase the sanctions against us. Every day, they threaten us. Every day, they strike us, and they don't honor what they have put in writing. So, if they engage in talks, will they actually follow through?"
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-trump-strait-of-hormuz-yemen-houthis-saudi-arabia/#post-update-0f461624
White House prepares plans to use weapon dubbed "mother of all bombs" on Iran, sources say
The White House and Pentagon have prepared plans to use a Massive Ordnance Air Blast bomb to hit military sites in Iran if ordered by President Trump, sources familiar with the matter told CBS News.
A Pentagon official told CBS News: "The Department of War plans for a wide range of operational scenarios to execute Presidential and Secretary level direction and to ensure mission success in any operational environment."
A White House spokesperson said: "We do not comment on sensitive military plans that may or may not exist. President Trump keeps all options on the table to ensure that Iran can never have a nuclear weapon."
Last week, Mr. Trump said the U.S. would not attack Iran before the midterm elections. He also touted "productive conversations" with Iran, though he did not provide specifics.
CNN first reported Thursday on the plans to use the Massive Ordnance Air Blast Bomb, or MOAB, on Iran.
Mr. Trump set a precedent for using the MOAB, also known as the "mother of all bombs," when it was used in 2017 to attack an Islamic State cave complex in Afghanistan. The bomb is the largest U.S. non-nuclear weapon, weighing upwards of 30,000 pounds at a cost of $170,000 per unit.

### 13. ★★★☆☆ Trump accuses Zelenskyy of not wanting to end the war sparked by Russia's full-scale invasion - CBS News

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1

- **发生了什么**: Trump accuses Zelenskyy of not wanting to end the war sparked by Russia's full-scale invasion&nbsp;&nbsp;CBS News

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

President Trump said Sunday that he is "in the process of negotiating Peace in the war between Russia and Ukraine ," and he accused Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy of not wanting to end the conflict sparked four and a half years ago by Russia's full-scale invasion.
In a Truth Social post on Sunday, Mr. Trump criticized Zelenskyy for targeting Russian diesel refineries and said, "he doesn't want to end this war, and probably never did."
"He could have made a better deal two years ago than he can now. His country is in shambles, a large portion of his land is lost, his oceans and seas are gone, and his male population is low, wounded, and/or dead," Mr. Trump wrote.
The comments come amid a deepening rift between Mr. Trump and Zelenskyy, with the U.S. president criticizing his Ukrainian counterpart for attacking Russian oil infrastructure &mdash; a tactic Ukrainian forces have focused on in recent months in a bid to deprive Putin's forces of both cash and fuel to continue their brutal offensive.
Smoke rises above the city following an air attack on the oil terminal in St. Petersburg on Oct. 18, 2026, amid the ongoing Russian-Ukrainian conflict.
Mr. Trump said Sunday that he had decided to hold off on implementing new sanctions against Russia because he doesn't "want to do anything that will drive, foolishly, Russia away from the bargaining table."
Mr. Trump asserted on Truth Social that "great progress is being made, and that includes current hostage negotiations," adding that, regarding the additional sanctions, "I can sign it later, or any time that I want!"
Zelenskyy earlier said that while he trusted Mr. Trump, siding with Moscow would be a tragedy and a "historic mistake" for Washington.
"People want to forget what's going on. But we can't give this possibility to forget, or to change reality, or to change the truth," Zelenskyy said. "We can't compare me and Putin. He's aggressor. He occupied us. He killed adults and children, his soldiers. He's a terrorist, and that's why I hope that President Trump will be very clear with this position."
Fishermen fish on the Neva River as smoke rises above the city following an air attack on the oil terminal in St. Petersburg on Oct. 18, 2026, amid the ongoing Russian-Ukrainian conflict.
Speaking Sunday on NBC's "Meet the Press," Zelenskyy said waiving sanctions against Russia , which Mr. Trump has already done to enable Russian diesel exports, would strengthen Putin's war effort. He also said U.S.-Ukraine intelligence sharing had resumed after being "blocked for some period of time."
Asked Sunday about reports, including by CBS News, that the U.S. may have intentionally withheld early warnings from Ukraine last week of deadly Russian missile strikes, and Zelenskyy's assertion that data sharing was blocked for a period of time, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said Sunday that he was "not sure Zelenskyy knows what he's talking about, but it wasn't deliberate."
Regarding separate but linked claims from Ukrainian officials that crucial satellite data from Elon Musk's Starlink network had been constrained last week, inhibiting Ukraine's offensive and defensive drone operations, Rubio said, "The United States, nobody ordered that Starlink speed to be slowed down. It sort of happened, but my understanding is it's been restored. It's back to where it was before."
Later Sunday, Zelenskyy said on social media that he had spoken with U.S. presidential envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner about the results of an Oct. 9 meeting between U.S. and Ukrainian negotiating teams in Miami, as well as "next steps in diplomacy."
Ukraine and Russia continued trading strikes over the weekend. Russia's Defense Ministry said Sunday it had intercepted more than 1,015 Ukrainian drones over 15 Russian regions, as well as Ukraine's Russian-occupied Crimean Peninsula and the Black Sea.
At least one person was killed and five others wounded in an attack on St. Petersburg that also sparked fires at the Petersburg Oil Terminal and the Ruchi oil depot, according to local government authorities.
People walk along dark streets during a power outage amid the Russia-Ukraine war and dropping temperatures in Lviv, Ukraine, on Oct. 17, 2026.
A logistics center belonging to Russian retailer Ozon and a data center operated by Russian tech giant VK were also targeted, the companies confirmed.
Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov told Russian state news agency Tass that Kyiv was "resisting" Moscow's invasion but could not "change the current course of events."
Meanwhile, in Ukraine, a woman was killed and a 6-year-old girl was among at least two other people wounded in two Russian attacks on the southern city of Zaporizhzhia, local officials said Sunday.

### 14. ★★★☆☆ Gold Prices Rise Above $4,200 in Futures Trading as Fed Rate Hike Expectations Ease - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T09:12:43+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Gold Prices Rise Above $4,200 in Futures Trading as Fed Rate Hike Expectations Ease&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Gold Prices Rise Above $4,200 in Futures Trading as Fed Rate Hike Expectations Ease
Gold bars ©Виталий Смолыгин Gold prices advanced on Monday as investors reduced expectations for another US Federal Reserve interest rate increase following comments from Chair Kevin Warsh. US gold futures moved above $4,200 an ounce, while other precious metals also recorded gains.
At 00:51 ET (04:51 GMT), spot gold rose 0.4% to $4,180.29 per ounce, while US gold futures increased 0.5% to $4,200.92.
The gains followed a 0.7% decline in bullion prices during the previous week, when stronger-than-expected US inflation data had reinforced expectations of further monetary tightening.
Investors were also assessing the outlook for the US dollar, government bond yields and demand for gold as a reserve and investment asset.
Warsh Comments Reduce Expectations for Fed Rate Increase
Market expectations for another Federal Reserve interest rate increase shifted following remarks from Chair Kevin Warsh.
Traders reduced the implied probability of a 25-basis-point increase at the central bank's next meeting to below 20%, interpreting Warsh's comments as consistent with keeping interest rates unchanged.
The reassessment followed US inflation figures released the previous week that exceeded market expectations.
Those figures had initially increased expectations of additional monetary tightening and supported the US dollar.
However, Warsh's subsequent remarks interrupted the dollar's advance and prompted investors to reconsider the likelihood of a near-term rate increase.
Higher interest rates can reduce the relative appeal of gold because the metal does not generate interest income.
Other precious metals recorded gains alongside gold during Monday's trading session.
Silver rose 1.3% to $57.7300 per ounce, while platinum increased 1.3% to $1,655.53.
Spot palladium advanced 1.5% to $1,132.74 per ounce.
Meanwhile, the US Dollar Index was broadly unchanged, declining 0.04% to 101.80.
The limited movement in the dollar followed the previous week's increase, which had been supported by expectations of tighter US monetary policy.
Precious metals markets continued to respond to changes in interest rate expectations, currency movements and broader investment demand.
Gold has remained above $4,000 per ounce since August, despite the effects of elevated bond yields and changes in expectations for US interest rates.
The metal previously entered bear-market territory following a decline associated with the outbreak of the US-Iran conflict in late February.
According to the supplied market report, gold remains approximately 20% below its level at the beginning of that conflict.
Demand for assets perceived as offering protection against geopolitical and financial uncertainty has provided some support to prices.
However, the opportunity cost of holding non-interest-bearing assets remains a consideration while government bond yields are elevated.
ANZ Highlights Fiscal Risks and Gold Investment Demand
Analysts at ANZ said concerns about government debt levels and fiscal sustainability were influencing the relationship between bond yields and gold prices.
According to the bank, higher sovereign borrowing costs may increasingly reflect concerns about public finances rather than expectations of stronger economic growth.
In this environment, investors may view gold as a means of diversifying exposure to financial and sovereign debt risks.
ANZ maintained a longer-term positive outlook for the metal while acknowledging that elevated bond yields and a relatively strong US dollar could place pressure on prices in the near term.
The analysts indicated that additional selling could occur before any sustained recovery.
ANZ also identified investment demand from China and reserve diversification by central banks as factors influencing the gold market.
According to the bank, Chinese investors have continued to allocate funds to gold amid subdued conditions in domestic equity and property markets.
Central banks have also increased efforts to diversify reserve holdings and reduce dependence on individual assets.
These sources of demand are relevant to the longer-term outlook for bullion, although their impact on prices will depend on broader financial market conditions.
ANZ expects continued investment and central-bank purchases to contribute to gold demand over the coming years.
ANZ expects gold prices to face limited upside potential in the near term before rising above $5,000 per ounce during 2027.
The forecast reflects the bank's assessment of investment demand, central-bank reserve diversification and fiscal conditions.
However, the projected increase remains dependent on the development of US monetary policy, the dollar and global bond yields.
For now, investors are assessing whether the Federal Reserve will maintain its current interest rate settings at its next meeting.
Gold futures traded above $4,200 per ounce on Monday, while spot prices remained slightly below that level.

</details>

### 18. ★★★☆☆ Two Oil Tankers Hit Mines While Crossing Strait of Hormuz - Marine Link

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Two Oil Tankers Hit Mines While Crossing Strait of Hormuz
Iran's Revolutionary Guards Navy said on Saturday that two oil tankers had caught fire after hitting naval mines while crossing the Strait of Hormuz, the official Iranian news agency IRNA reported.
State media said both tankers were carrying about 2 million barrels of crude oil.
There was no independent confirmation of the reports.
IRNA reported that the vessels had switched off their positioning and radar systems and attempted to use an unauthorised route through the strategic waterway. The second vessel was left immobilised after striking a mine, it added.
State media earlier reported that the other tanker had also struck a mine while attempting to transit the strait via a route not authorised by Iran.
The incidents come amid heightened tensions around the strait, a key oil shipping route. Disruptions to shipping in the waterway due to the US-Israeli war on Iran have boosted global energy prices and inflation.
Maritime officials said on Wednesday that a container ship and a dry bulk carrier sank near the strait after being hit by projectiles in separate incidents.
Attacks around the waterway have surged over the past two weeks, with Iran saying it is stepping up strikes in response to the US blockade of its ports and tougher sanctions imposed last month.
(Reuters - Reporting by Menna Alaa El-Din. Additional reporting by Dubai newsroom. Editing by Sam Holmes and Mark Potter)

### 20. ★★★☆☆ A renewed Iran war is only a matter of time, Israeli experts warn - Jewish Telegraphic Agency

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T08:49:33+00:00
- **发生了什么**: A renewed Iran war is only a matter of time, Israeli experts warn&nbsp;&nbsp;Jewish Telegraphic Agency

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

A renewed Iran war is only a matter of time, Israeli experts warn
In a regional stalemate, Iran controls the Strait of Hormuz, while the U.S. applies economic pressure.
A giant anti-Israel and anti-Netanyahu billboard is displayed at Enghelab Square in Tehran as tensions between Iran and the United States and Israel continue, on October 12, 2026 in Tehran, Iran. The billboard depicts Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu addressing an empty hall and carries Persian messages reading, 'More isolated with each passing day' and 'Al-Aqsa Flood is a definite defeat for the Zionist regime.' (Fatemeh Bahrami/Anadolu via Getty Images)
President Donald Trump and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have said they don’t want to see a renewal of the war against Iran – but they are issuing stark public warnings to Tehran that could bring the simmering conflict to a boil.
Trump endorsed Netanyahu for Prime Minister on Sunday, explaining that his leadership was critical to any renewed Iran campaign.
A third U.S. aircraft carrier is expected to arrive in the Middle East soon, amid signs that Washington is preparing for a possible resumption of hostilities, even though Israel’s Oct. 27 election and the American midterms on Nov. 3 are looming.
In multiple interviews, including with Channel 14, Netanyahu stressed that, “If Iran makes a mistake and tries to attack us, it will simply make the mistake of its life.” But he added, “It’s not convenient for us” for war to resume weeks before a national election in a country with no absentee voting.
Trump, for his part, told reporters in Atlanta last week, “Wait til you see what we do to Iran.”
Israeli experts have warned that renewed conflict is only a matter of time as an uneasy standoff has continued, with Iran disrupting shipping through the Strait of Hormuz from where 20% of the world’s oil flows. At the same time, the U.S. is maintaining its economic pressure on Tehran.
High oil prices have risen 46% to $104 per barrel since the Feb. 28 attacks, pushing gasoline prices up 46% to $4.36 per gallon.That increase could give Trump another incentive for another strike, which would renew the war that was paused in April.
“Without a diplomatic settlement, the chances of escalation are very high,” Danny Citrinowicz, a senior researcher at the Institute for National Security Studies, told JTA.
Trump has been unable to achieve a diplomatic agreement. Both Tehran and Washington believe they won the two-month war earlier this year. In practice, however, the Islamic regime is still in power with capacity to recoup its nuclear and ballistic missile losses and continues to exert some control in the Strait. All of which makes it difficult for Israel and the U.S. to show that they won.
“Trump cannot declare victory now, but he also cannot leave without doing something,” said Citrinowicz, who was also the former head of the Iran branch in the Israeli military intelligence directorate’s Research and Analysis Division. “On the other hand, the Iranians cannot allow the naval blockade to continue, so they will keep attacking tankers.”
Avner Vilan, a former senior Israeli security official and an expert on Iran, said “Trump has said he won’t attack before the midterm elections, and I think he has good reasons to hold off.”
He stressed, however, that the risk of escalation certainly exists. “We’re in a situation where both sides continue to exert pressure on each other, without any lasting agreement in place,” he said. “Everyone is on edge, and all it would take is one significant incident to send things spiraling out of control.”
Gil Murciano, CEO of Mitvim, the Israeli Institute for Regional Foreign Policies, said the unpopularity of the war with Iran among the American public makes a U.S.-initiated escalation before the midterm elections less likely. A Pew Research Center poll released last week found that 64% of Americans disapprove of how President Donald Trump is handling U.S. military action against Iran.
A poll conducted by the Institute for National Security Studies last month found that Israelis are divided over whether Israel should launch a preemptive strike on Iran, with opponents slightly outnumbering supporters.
The New York Times reported that officials in the Trump administration had discussed a short, intense campaign lasting about three days, focused on Iran’s missile and drone arsenal, potentially alongside strikes on energy infrastructure and Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps headquarters.
The Atlantic reported that the White House has asked the Pentagon to develop strike options against Iranian targets that could be carried out ahead of the midterm elections.
“A three-day campaign that would, for example, include a strike on Pickaxe Mount would allow Trump to say that he had obliterated Iran’s nuclear project, won the war and was ending the conflict,” Citrinowicz said. “That is one option. Another is to strike Iranian infrastructure facilities.” He cautioned, however, that even a planned three-day campaign could lead to a much broader and more prolonged escalation.
Mitvim’s Murciano said the main factor that could lead the Trump administration and Israel to launch another military campaign is the concern that “Iran could rebuild its capacity to produce surface-to-surface missiles and restore its drone capabilities in a way that would allow it to attack Israel and other countries in the region, as well as strengthen its ability to disrupt shipping in the Strait of Hormuz.”
He added that another key concern for Israel and the U.S. is the possibility of Iran making a breakthrough toward developing a nuclear weapon.
Vilan, the former Israeli security official, stressed that Israel’s interests do not fully align with those of the U.S.. “For Israel, a nuclear-armed Iran would pose a paramount strategic threat, and Iran must not be allowed to acquire nuclear weapons,” he said.
“For Trump, preventing Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon is an important goal, but he also wants to end the war, reopen the Strait of Hormuz and bring down energy prices,” he added. “Another military campaign could actually take him further away from those objectives.”
Trump and other administration officials have said publicly they believe Iran is at a point of weakness. “Trump may think that Iran is so weak that just one more push will make it collapse. I think that is a mistake,” Citrinowicz said.
The analysts believe an escalation is more likely after the midterm elections.
Murciano noted that if Trump loses the midterm elections and loses his ability to shape domestic policy without control of Congress, he may shift his focus toward cementing his foreign policy legacy.
Vilan cautioned that while some of the political constraints on Trump may ease after the midterm elections, that does not necessarily make another military campaign against Iran worthwhile.
“If the goal is to force Iran into a nuclear deal, it needs to be explained why another round of strikes would achieve what previous strikes have not,” he said. “It is hard to see how additional military force would change Iran’s position. If the goal is to bring down the regime, it is equally unclear how another round of strikes would bring that about — and, in particular, whether it could be achieved in the next two years, before a new president takes office in the White House,” the former senior Israeli security official said.
Iran could also initiate an escalation itself if it believes that Israel or the U.S. is preparing to attack, said Citrinowicz.
“It does not want to be caught by surprise for a third time. The likelihood of that happening is higher than it was in the past, but the Iranians still prefer a gradual escalation over a broad and immediate one,” he said.
Vilan added that “the Iranian regime is facing a dilemma. On the one hand, the economic pressure on it is immense, and time is not necessarily on its side. On the other hand, it knows that an all-out war could threaten its survival.”
Another factor that could come into play is the Houthis, Iran-backed rebels in Yemen who have seized significant territory along the Red Sea coast in recent weeks and disrupted shipping through the Bab el-Mandeb Strait, adding to pressure on the global economy.
“The Houthis are a very important factor in the regional conflict, and their recent gains illustrate the limits of American military superiority,” Vilan said.
Their impact on the prospects of another campaign against Iran could cut both ways, he added. “As the campaign in Yemen becomes more complicated, the Americans have less incentive to open another front. On the other hand, a major escalation by the Houthis could draw the U.S. into a broader military engagement, potentially bringing an escalation with Iran closer.”
Meanwhile, diplomatic contacts between the U.S. and Iran continue through intermediaries. The New York Times reported that the White House believes progress has been made in the talks.

### 21. ★★★☆☆ US Stock Futures Rise Ahead of Technology Earnings as Oil Prices Decline: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T09:09:12+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US Stock Futures Rise Ahead of Technology Earnings as Oil Prices Decline: Dow Jones, S&amp;P, Nasdaq, Wall Street&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US Stock Futures Rise Ahead of Technology Earnings as Oil Prices Decline: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street
Trading floor of the New York stock exchange ©Carol M. Highsmith Archive, Library of Congress, Prints and Photographs Division US stock futures moved higher on Monday as investors prepared for quarterly earnings from major technology companies amid concerns about artificial intelligence investment and financing conditions. Oil prices declined following US President Donald Trump's decision to waive certain sanctions on Russia, while Chinese economic data showed an acceleration in third-quarter growth.
Futures linked to the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500 and Nasdaq 100 advanced in early trading, following declines in the main Wall Street indices on Friday.
Investors were also assessing preliminary financial results from Chinese memory chip manufacturer ChangXin Memory Technologies (CXMT), whose shares declined despite forecasts of substantial revenue growth and a return to profitability.
US Futures Advance Following Technology Sector Declines
At 03:06 ET (07:06 GMT), Dow Jones futures had risen 215 points, or 0.4%, while S&P 500 futures gained 53 points, or 0.7%.
Nasdaq 100 futures increased 384 points, equivalent to 1.3%.
The gains followed a decline in US equities on Friday, when concerns about financing requirements and valuations across the artificial intelligence sector affected technology stocks.
Analysts at Vital Knowledge described investor sentiment towards AI-related equities as "considerably darker".
The firm highlighted concerns about the sustainability of debt and equity issuance used to finance the expansion of AI infrastructure.
Media reports indicating that quarterly revenue growth at Anthropic, the developer of the Claude AI model, remained substantial but had slowed compared with the preceding quarter also contributed to investor attention on the sector.
Vital Knowledge analysts said: "While many stocks are still above their summer lows, the psychology towards the group seems worse than it was in June and July, due in large part to the dramatic worsening in certain pockets of the AI credit market."
Analysts at ING also examined the potential implications of technology market volatility for the US dollar.
The bank said the dollar "could be a laggard in further stock market instability", although it did not anticipate an imminent substantial decline in the currency.
The assessment reflects concerns that changes in investor positioning towards AI-related assets could affect other financial markets.
Investors are continuing to evaluate the financing requirements of major technology companies and the potential relationship between infrastructure expenditure, revenue growth and future profitability.
Quarterly earnings from major US technology companies will be a focus during the week, particularly as investors assess spending on artificial intelligence.
Meta Platforms (NASDAQ:META) is scheduled to report its results after the US market close on Wednesday.
The company has expanded its AI activities following the September launch of its Muse model, which reportedly attracted millions of downloads.
The technology includes services designed to assist users with travel bookings and small-business tasks.
Meta has also released an open-weight version of its Muse Spark model for coding applications.
Investors will be examining Meta's financial performance and management commentary on AI investment, capital expenditure and the commercial development of its technology.
Tesla (NASDAQ:TSLA) is also scheduled to publish quarterly results after Wednesday's closing bell.
The company recently attracted attention following a live demonstration in which a booster-powered vehicle completed a controlled landing.
Both earnings announcements come during a period of increased scrutiny of technology companies' investment programmes and growth expectations.
Oil Prices Fall Following US Decision on Russian Sanctions
Crude oil prices declined on Monday after President Donald Trump issued an executive order suspending numerous sanctions on Russia shortly before their scheduled implementation.
The administration also waived proposed tariffs on countries importing Russian oil.
At 03:56 ET, Brent crude futures fell 2.6% to $106.97 per barrel, while US West Texas Intermediate (WTI) futures declined 2.0% to $99.36 per barrel.
The declines followed a gain of nearly 5% in crude prices during the previous week, when concerns about supply disruptions, particularly in the Middle East, supported the market.
The US measures raised expectations that additional Russian crude could remain available to international buyers.
Trump subsequently indicated on social media that maintaining Russia's participation in negotiations was among the considerations behind his approach to efforts to end the war in Ukraine.
Earlier, the president had said that no final decision had been made, while US Secretary of State Marco Rubio stated that the administration could take up to 60 days to assess the economic effects of the waivers.
Chinese memory chip manufacturer ChangXin Memory Technologies forecast a substantial increase in revenue for the first nine months of 2026, citing demand associated with artificial intelligence and higher memory chip prices.
In a preliminary earnings estimate, CXMT projected revenue of between 265 billion yuan and 270 billion yuan, equivalent to approximately $39.6 billion to $40.4 billion.
The forecast compares with revenue of 32.08 billion yuan in the corresponding period of 2025, representing an increase of more than eight times.
The company also expects net profit attributable to shareholders of between 131 billion yuan and 132.5 billion yuan.
This would represent a reversal from the net loss of 5.28 billion yuan recorded in the first nine months of the previous year.
Despite the projected improvement in financial performance, CXMT shares declined.
The share price movement came amid heightened investor expectations for companies benefiting from demand for AI-related semiconductor products.
China's Third-Quarter GDP Growth Accelerates to 4.5%
China's economy expanded faster than expected in the third quarter of 2026, supported by exports and demand associated with artificial intelligence.
Data from the National Bureau of Statistics showed gross domestic product increased 4.5% year on year in the three months ended September 30.
The result exceeded economists' expectations for 4.4% growth and represented an acceleration from the 4.3% annual increase recorded in the preceding quarter.
On a quarter-on-quarter basis, GDP grew 1.2%, compared with forecasts of 1.1% and growth of 0.9% in the second quarter.
For the first nine months of 2026, China's economy expanded by 4.6%.
The figures place growth within the government's previously stated annual target range of 4.5% to 5%.
Investors Monitor Earnings, Energy Markets and Chinese Growth
Financial markets began the week with gains in US stock futures, lower crude oil prices and further developments in the technology sector.
The upcoming earnings announcements from Meta and Tesla will provide additional information about corporate performance and investment plans.
At the same time, changes to US sanctions on Russia are influencing expectations for global oil supply, while China's economic data and CXMT's preliminary results provide further indications of activity in the Chinese economy and semiconductor industry.

### 23. ★★★☆☆ Inside the shadowy world of anti-Putin sabotage from within Russia

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T00:06:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Two men tell of the work they say they have been doing to support Ukrainian intelligence during the war.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Watch: Russian saboteur working for Ukraine shares reconnaissance videos
Two Russian men secretly working for Ukraine&#x27;s intelligence services have given rare accounts of operations they claim to have been involved with, that have targeted military assets and personnel inside Russia.
One of the men, whom we are calling Ivan, claims to have played a role from Russia in Ukrainian drone attacks on strategic bombers and munitions factories on his home soil.
He features in a BBC World Service documentary about the shadowy world of Russian saboteurs.
The documentary, by independent Polish journalist Marcin Mamon, also features a man we are calling Foma, a Russian living in exile in Poland.
Foma runs a network of undercover volunteers in Russia, that surveils high-level officers that the GUR - Ukraine&#x27;s military intelligence service - is considering targeting, he says. He stresses they are never involved in the attacks themselves.
He was also tight-lipped about who they were surveilling, but after Lt Gen Fanil Sarvarov was assassinated in December last year in Moscow , Foma admitted he had provided the Ukrainians with intelligence on him.
Image caption, Lt Gen Fanil Sarvarov was killed in Moscow last year by an explosive device hidden under his car
There have been previous media reports of Russian sabotage groups, but it is rare to get access to someone operating on the ground, like Ivan, explaining alleged activities.
The GUR has confirmed that Ivan and Foma are both working for the agency. But it also has acknowledged that it cannot know the full picture. When Ivan&#x27;s handler was pressed on whether he could be trusted, the handler replied: "I&#x27;m not sure of anyone, except myself."
And both the GUR and the men are cautious about how much detail they share, making it impossible to verify the activities the men claim to have carried out.
The broad tactics Ivan and Foma describe, however, have been acknowledged by the Ukrainian authorities. Kyiv has spoken of its strategy of co-ordinated drone attacks on what it claims are legitimate targets inside Russia, and has acknowledged it uses secret agents for this.
There have also been several political assassinations in Russia since the start of the war. As a matter of policy, Ukraine never officially admits or claims responsibility for targeted attacks, though a Ukrainian intelligence officer has previously admitted to the BBC it was behind the killing of senior military figure Lt Gen Igor Kirillov in December 2024.
It is difficult to confirm how many Russians are doing this kind of work for Ukraine. According to Foma, they number about 300-500, though this is impossible to verify.
If you are outside the UK, watch on YouTube , external and listen to the BBC World Service Documentary Podcast - Shadowmen: Russians working with Ukraine
Ivan and Foma say they are both opposed not just to Russia&#x27;s war in Ukraine, but to President Vladimir Putin&#x27;s regime - and believe his political future depends on the war&#x27;s outcome.
"At the moment... I think the [Putin] regime might fall if they lose the warâ¦ Through our actions... we are trying to speed this up," Ivan says.
His work, he told Mamon, involved surveilling military sites, and locating places to hide drone components, in preparation for potential attacks on Russian assets.
"When these [drone] components are combinedâ¦ it turns out a formidable weapon, which with a single blow can destroy an object that costs many, many millions of dollars," Ivan said.
He sent Mamon a video which showed him apparently hiding explosives near a Russian military airfield.
If he had been caught, he would have been charged with terrorism, and jailed for decades.
Foma&#x27;s work is different - he operates remotely, training his volunteers in Russia, online. Mamon watched him conducting one such tutorial - on how to trail a senior military official marked for surveillance.
Image caption, Foma explained how he taught his undercover volunteers to trail Russian officers
"You will get a file... from me or a colleagueâ¦ with the proposed target. The file will contain [the target&#x27;s] photo, his data, his details, date of birth, age, and a description of him. There&#x27;ll be information on his car, if he has one. When you go to the target, don&#x27;t take a phone," he told his volunteers.
Foma was once part of the Russian elite, so he claims to have a unique set of contacts and constant flow of insider information.
He showed Mamon on his laptop how he mapped military targets.
"This shows a database... the location of storage warehouses, and military units. Where generals liveâ¦ Where the pilots of strategic bombers who shell Ukraine live.
"In order to break the system, you need to understand how it works. Then you can aim for their weak points."
He said there were about "three dozen" people in his network split between Russia, Ukraine and elsewhere in Europe, with the contingent outside Russia providing logistical support, such as tracking targets&#x27; digital footprints.
Foma stressed that the GUR suggests the targets to be surveilled, but that he and his team check for themselves that these individuals should be considered military criminals.
"As a ruleâ¦ we also check the data [on that person]â¦ Only after that we say whether we work or not."
But he argued that his team&#x27;s actions were justified.
"I want people who do terrible things on behalf of Putin and Russia in Ukraineâ¦ to understand that they&#x27;ll have to pay for it. I want them to get scared."
Foma stressed that he only passed on intelligence to the Ukrainians, and his network did not carry out any assassinations themselves.
Of the assassination of Gen Sarvarov, killed in December 2025, he later texted Mamon to say: "Keeping track of him took quite an effort. He lived alternately with his mistress and his colleague. Covered his tracks and constantly changed his schedule."
But Foma did not share any plans with Mamon and the BBC in advance.
Ivan, however, was tight-lipped about the work he had been involved with, even after the event.
After checking with his military intelligence contacts in both Ukraine and Russia, he thought it likely that Ivan had been involved in a plan to strike Russia&#x27;s largest military drone factory at Yelabuga in June last year , external . One person was reported to have been killed in the incident.
Ivan stressed that the aim is always to try to avoid harming civilians.
"The most serious case was when my car was stopped for inspection and I had the handmade explosives on me. The risk was very high - however as you can see, I was not detected," he said.
To mitigate the risk of being compromised, "there is no clear structure" to the work, he said, adding he had never met and did not know the identity of others he had been working alongside.
"As soon as you start gathering people in groups, in general chatsâ¦ leaving a mark on the internet, with a high probability this whole group will be quickly detained or destroyed," he said.
Ivan stressed there were few people left in Russia suitable for this kind of work.
"Most of the ideological people have either left the country or are already behind bars," he said.
Many Russians involved in sabotage work on their native soil are therefore either oblivious - for example lorry drivers unaware that their cargoes have been compromised - or are individuals who have been paid, the BBC understands.
Ivan, on the other hand, says he is doing this work for ideological reasons - he used to be a member of the political opposition. As such, his movements would be expected to be monitored closely by the Russian authorities.
So it was a surprise that he managed to frequently leave Russia and return.
Image caption, Marcin waiting to meet Ivan in Georgia - we were surprised by Ivan&#x27;s apparent freedom to travel
Mamon persuaded Ivan to drive him into the Georgian desert, where they could speak freely - and asked him how the Ukrainians could trust him.
"You go to a third countryâ¦ where [Ukraine&#x27;s] agents meet youâ¦ and conduct lie detector tests," Ivan told him.
Mamon asked Ivan to put him in touch with one of his GUR handlers in Ukraine. This man immediately rang Ivan on speaker phone - apparently demonstrating that Ivan worked for him. They spoke in code, referring to "repairs at the school".
But later, the handler admitted he could not rule out the possibility Ivan might work for the Russians too, either voluntarily or under duress.
Foma thinks the fact that Ivan can travel freely does not necessarily constitute a red flag.
"There are all sorts of holes in [the country&#x27;s governance], and if you know them, or are just lucky, then you can jump through the holesâ¦ and live in the system. Like a mouse in a piece of cheese," he told Mamon.
Perhaps more compellingly, if Ivan had also been spying for FSB, it would seem likely that he would share what he knew of Ukraine&#x27;s attack plans with the Russian agency. More than a year before a devastating strike by more than 100 Ukrainian drones, Ivan had been telling Marcin there would be a major attack on strategic bombers on military airfields across Russia, though he never said when or where.
"Fear is always present," he said. "It follows me everywhere. Even if you don&#x27;t do anything, you&#x27;re still afraid. This is Russia."
But he was optimistic that eventually his life would change.

</details>
