### 24. ★★★☆☆ Australian Greens senator denies claims of misconduct towards women

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T04:54:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Jordon Steele-John says the timing of the claims is motivated by "nasty political games" within the Greens.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Greens senator Jordon Steele-John denies claims of misconduct against women
An Australian Greens senator has denied allegations of inappropriate behaviour towards women and accused his party of "nasty political games", days after the claims emerged.
Last Thursday, Greens leader David Shoebridge said Senator Jordon Steele-John, 32, had taken leave from the party room after a letter from seven current and former members said he had been accused of "multiple allegations" of misconduct between 2017 and 2025.
In an online post on Sunday, the senator denied the claims and said he was not on leave. On a sexual assault claim, he said police investigating the matter had closed the case and no charges were laid.
An internal party probe has been launched into the allegations.
Steele-John, who has cerebral palsy and uses a wheelchair, was first elected to the senate in Western Australia in November 2017.
In his letter responding to the claims, Steele-John said his nickname - "Feels on wheels" - initially referred to him being emotional when advocating for disability rights but had since taken on a "more sinister meaning" about his alleged mistreatment of women.
He accused Shoebridge of mishandling the matter and questioned the timing of the letter, saying "it is no coincidence" that the allegations were sent to the leader "the day after we agreed on the timeline for the next preselection" ahead of the next federal election.
Days after the letter with the claims was sent to Shoebridge, local media reported that the party had created a 47-page dossier years earlier that listed a number of allegations against Steele-John and how to manage media coverage if they emerged.
Steele-John said the dossier was made so that the media would not "bombard" the two women at the centre of those claims and that neither woman had made a complaint.
He acknowledged the allegations against him would have "damaged" the public&#x27;s trust in him and said he was "committed to rebuilding that by acting with as much transparency and accountability as I can".
Shoebridge&#x27;s office confirmed that the allegations had been referred to the Independent Parliamentary Standards Commission.
A spokesperson for Shoebridge said the leader still believed "leave is appropriate under the circumstances" and taking leave would not influence the internal party investigation.

</details>

### 26. ★★★☆☆ Bank of America Identifies Technoprobe and Soitec as Preferred European Semiconductor Stocks - Yahoo Finance UK

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T08:39:14+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Bank of America Identifies Technoprobe and Soitec as Preferred European Semiconductor Stocks&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance UK

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Bank of America Identifies Technoprobe and Soitec as Preferred European Semiconductor Stocks
Semiconductor ©Adobe Stock Images Bank of America has identified Technoprobe (BIT:TPRO) and Soitec (EU:SOI) as its preferred stocks among European small- and mid-cap semiconductor companies, citing their exposure to artificial intelligence infrastructure investment.
The bank's assessment focuses on two areas of semiconductor technology: advanced processor testing and data centre connectivity.
Both companies have received upgrades from Neutral to Buy, reflecting Bank of America's expectations for revenue growth, capacity expansion and improved operating margins.
The analysts expect investment by major cloud service providers in AI computing infrastructure to support demand for the technologies supplied by the two companies.
Bank of America upgraded Technoprobe from Neutral to Buy in May, identifying the Italian semiconductor testing equipment company as having the highest AI-related revenue exposure among the European small- and mid-cap semiconductor companies it covers.
According to the bank, approximately 50% of Technoprobe's current revenue is linked to artificial intelligence applications.
BofA expects this proportion to increase to 70% by 2028, supported by demand for more complex semiconductor testing and developments in high-bandwidth memory (HBM).
Technoprobe supplies probe boards used in semiconductor testing, including products based on vertical microelectromechanical systems (MEMS) technology.
The bank highlighted the company's position in graphics processing unit (GPU) probe boards and expects increasing testing requirements for central processing units (CPUs), application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs), HBM and silicon photonics to support its business.
Bank of America also pointed to Technoprobe's 2026 financial guidance, which it said exceeded median market consensus estimates by 33% for revenue and 65% for EBITDA.
Following the company's guidance, the bank raised its revenue growth forecast for the 2026–2028 period from 40% to 48%.
Its EBITDA growth forecast for the same period increased from 70% to 78%.
These revisions reflect the analysts' expectations for additional demand associated with AI semiconductor testing and the potential contribution from higher-bandwidth memory applications.
The projections remain Bank of America's estimates and are subject to future industry demand and company performance.
Bank of America upgraded French semiconductor materials manufacturer Soitec from Neutral to Buy on October 9.
The bank's assessment centres on silicon photonics, a technology used to support high-speed optical data transmission in computing infrastructure.
According to BofA, Soitec holds approximately 95% of the silicon-on-insulator (SOI) wafer market serving photonics applications.
The analysts expect demand for optical connectivity in data centres to increase as AI infrastructure requires higher data transmission capacity.
BofA believes growth in optical port requirements could exceed growth in AI processor volumes, reflecting the increasing bandwidth requirements of large-scale computing systems.
This trend is expected to support demand for SOI wafers used in silicon photonics applications.
Bank of America expects improved demand visibility to support a potential announcement by Soitec concerning additional manufacturing capacity in Singapore over the coming months.
The bank believes such an expansion could lead the company to revise its financial targets higher.
However, the anticipated capacity announcement remains an analyst expectation rather than a confirmed company decision.
BofA also acknowledged that Soitec's share price has increased substantially during 2026.
As a result, its Buy recommendation is based primarily on expectations for capacity growth and margin expansion rather than the stock's current valuation.
AI Infrastructure Investment Underpins Both Recommendations
Bank of America's selections reflect two different areas of semiconductor infrastructure investment.
Technoprobe provides testing technology for advanced processors and memory devices, while Soitec supplies semiconductor materials used in optical connectivity.
The bank expects demand for both technologies to develop as cloud computing companies expand their AI infrastructure.
For Technoprobe, the principal factors include higher testing complexity, increasing AI-related revenue exposure and projected EBITDA growth.
For Soitec, the assessment focuses on silicon photonics demand, potential manufacturing expansion and the resulting implications for operating margins.
Both recommendations depend on the continuation of investment in AI infrastructure and the companies' ability to deliver the anticipated financial performance.

</details>

### 33. ★★★☆☆ Musk and Anthropic pledge public share giveaways

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T09:38:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Musk's announcement comes after Anthropic pledged to create a public benefit fund ahead of its blockbuster stock market listing.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

By Peter Hoskins Â  and Â  Michael Race ,Â  Business reporters
Elon Musk&#x27;s SpaceX and artificial intelligence giant Anthropic have pledged big financial giveaways as Big Tech firms come under pressure over the potential impact of AI on humanity.
Musk said on Monday that he would give one of his shares at his rocket company, which owns xAI, to every child in America next year.
The billionaire&#x27;s announcement came after Anthropic said it would commit 5% of its shares to a fund that will make donations for the "public good".
Anthropic is laying the ground for a potential blockbuster stock market listing that could value the firm at more than $1tn (Â£750bn). SpaceX set the record for biggest initial public offering (IPO) in history in June, raising $85.7bn.
The pledges come at a time when the AI industry is under intense scrutiny over safety concerns and fears about its impact on society and jobs.
Details over how each American child would be handed a share in SpaceX have not been disclosed.
In a post on social media, , external Musk said: "Next year, I will give one of my SpaceX shares to every kid in America. This will be the first of many."
It came after he responded to Anthropic&#x27;s public fund pledge with: "I recommend giving the shares directly to the public. This is what I will be doing."
Anthropic said none of the shares held in its Public Benefit Fund would be used for lobbying or political donations in the US or overseas.
The Claude developer said on Sunday that the fund could also receive another 2% of the firm&#x27;s shares at its board&#x27;s discretion.
Last week, Anthropic told prospective investors that its revenue surged by 45% to $16.8bn (Â£12.6bn) in the three months to the end of September.
In common with other AI firms, it is seeking to position itself as being able to generate huge profits despite spending billions on developing the technology and building infrastructure to power its models.
Companies around the world, primarily technology firms, have cut thousands of jobs after investing in AI, with many pointing to higher efficiencies from its adoption.
Research by the International Monetary Fund (IMF) published in 2024 suggested that almost 40% of jobs around the world will be affected by AI , with that figure rising as high as â60% in advanced economies.
In September, a researcher who quit Anthropic said people working on the technology were "genuinely frightened" about the speed of its advancements and what it could mean for humanity.
"I believe that if we don&#x27;t slow down at the current rate of progress, there is a strong chance that we could all die in the immediate future," he said.
Jacob Coxon was speaking to the BBC after his resignation post on the dangers of AI went viral on a backdrop of growing safety concerns about the industry.
The 27-year-old&#x27;s former boss, Anthropic head Dario Amodei, had previously called for AI development to slow down - though some have questioned the motivations behind this.
The bosses of two rival AI firms, Sam Altman of OpenAI and Musk, have both said they agree with Amodei&#x27;s proposal for industry-wide deceleration and regulation, as well as independent monitoring of AI model development.
Earlier this month, US President Donald Trump and the heads of major AI firms signed a "morally binding" document , making them responsible for ensuring the safety of their own technology.
Trump has repeatedly rejected the idea of laws to control development of the technology despite a debate about the potential risks of AI and what it means for humanity.
Since then, the president has named the director of national intelligence Jay Clayton as the head of a new taskforce on AI which, he said, will co-ordinate government engagement with consumers, companies and "religious organizations".
Why are there concerns AI could threaten humanity, and how real are they?
Anthropic bans users from being &#x27;cruel&#x27; to its AI systems

### 34. ★★★☆☆ Trump says suspension of Russia sanctions is to keep peace negotiations going

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-18T23:20:15+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The US president blocked numerous sanctions on Russia two days before they were due to take effect.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US President Donald Trump has said his decision to suspend several Russian sanctions is an effort to keep Moscow at the negotiating table.
"I am in the process of negotiating Peace in the war between Russia and Ukraine," Trump said on his Truth Social messaging platform, adding that he didn&#x27;t want to "do anything that will drive, foolishly, Russia away from the bargaining table".
Trump blocked numerous sanctions on Russia on Friday night, two days before they were due to take effect, as a row with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky deepened.
Zelensky told NBC News on Sunday that siding with Russian President Vladimir Putin would be a "historic mistake".
Russian envoy Kirill Dmitriev has responded to Trump&#x27;s order by calling it "an important and truly historic moment". He said the sanctions, which were passed by the US Congress and signed into law by the US president in September, would have inflicted "serious damage" on the US and global market.
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio has described the suspension of the sanctions as a 60-day pause during which their potential impact on the US and global economies would be analysed.
In his Truth Social post, Trump also said Zelensky "doesn&#x27;t want to end this war, and probably never did", adding "he could have made a better deal two years ago than he can now".
Zelensky has pushed for an end to fighting, and has repeatedly said that Putin doesn&#x27;t want peace, since Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.
Relations between Washington and Kyiv frayed after the US struck a controversial deal to import Russian diesel last week.
Despite the international sanctions, Russia has still been able to export oil, much of it to China and India - often at attractive discounts. According to the think-tank Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air (CREA), China has taken about half of Russia&#x27;s crude exports, with India accounting for about 37%.
In remarks to a European Union summit, after Trump announced the deal to buy diesel from Russia and said Zelensky was "more difficult to deal with" than Putin, Zelensky said it was nice to be among "real leaders".
Image caption, Ukraine&#x27;s President Zelensky attended an EU leaders&#x27; summit in Brussels on Friday
Zelensky has repeatedly pushed for stringent sanctions to limit funding that Russia can use in its war effort. After the latest move from the US, however, he said he believed the Trump administration would lift more sanctions against Russia.
Ukraine and its European allies criticised Trump&#x27;s plans to import Russian diesel to help lower US energy prices, which have skyrocketed during the Iran war.
The deal, according to Zelensky, was tantamount to the US investing in Russia&#x27;s war effort.
Concerns that Trump is siding with Russia have been heightened by disruption to Starlink, a US-provided satellite system used by Ukraine. That hampered Kyiv&#x27;s ability to protect itself from Russian attacks.
Zelensky said US officials had linked speed restrictions to technical issues.
However, he said Ukraine had tested Starlink on EU territory and found it was operating at higher speeds.
Trump has denied both deliberately slowing down the network and a further accusation from Zelensky that the US had stopped sharing defence intelligence.
Trump waives several Russia sanctions as row with Zelensky intensifies
Zelensky says Ukraine has right to defend itself after Trump calls latest strikes a &#x27;disgrace&#x27;
Russia accuses Ukraine of attacking oil refinery after Zelensky says strikes to resume

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Oil prices slide as sanctions relief on Russian crude raises hopes of increased supply
Crude oil prices fell Monday as traders weighed possibly higher Russian supplies hitting the market.
U.S. President Donald Trump invoked national-interest to suspend new duties on Russian energy buyers.
Smoke rises above the city following an air attack on the oil terminal in Saint Petersburg on October 18, 2026, amid the ongoing Russian-Ukrainian conflict.
Crude oil prices fell Monday as traders weighed possibly higher Russian supplies hitting the market after U.S. sanctions relief on Friday.
Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, fell 2.38% to $107.25 per barrel. U.S. West Texas intermediate lost 1.78% to $99.60 per barrel.
Ukraine bombing of oil facilities in Russia and more tankers coming under attack in the Persian Gulf, however, have clouded the supply outlook.
Fires broke out at an oil terminal and depot in St. Petersburg after Ukraine launched a massive wave of drone strikes on Sunday, St. Petersburg Governor Alexander Beglov said in a post on Telegram.
The terminal in St. Petersburg exports about 120,000 barrels per day of refined products, mostly fuel oil, according to data from Kpler. It is unclear how bad the damage is at the facility.
Ukraine briefly paused strikes on Russian energy facilities after U.S. President Donald Trump declared on Oct. 11 that Kyiv and Moscow had agreed to a truce .
Heavy smoke billows after Ukrainian drones hit infrastructure, according to local authorities, in St Petersburg, Russia June 3, 2026.
In the Middle East, meanwhile, two tankers were attacked off the coast of Saudi Arabia in the Persian Gulf on Friday, according to incident reports from the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations Centre.
A small boat fired a rocket-propelled grenade at an unidentified tanker 78 nautical miles northeast of Al Jubayl, according to UKMTO. Another tanker, the ST3, was hit by an unknown projectile, UKMTO reported.
Iran has escalated attacks on tankers in the Persian Gulf, Strait of Hormuz and Gulf of Oman in an effort to choke off crude oil exports from the region.

### 36. ★★★☆☆ Trump endorses embattled Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu for reelection

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: NPR World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T05:35:39+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Trump said in a post on social media that Netanyahu "has been a Great Warrior Prime Minister, and an even greater Partner for Peace in combatting the Islamic Republic of Iran."

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Trump endorses embattled Israeli PM Netanyahu for reelection Trump said in a post on social media that Netanyahu "has been a Great Warrior Prime Minister, and an even greater Partner for Peace in combatting the Islamic Republic of Iran."
Trump endorses embattled Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu for reelection
FILE - President Donald Trump listens as Israel's Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu speaks at President Donald Trump's Mar-a-Lago club, Dec. 29, 2025, in Palm Beach, Fla.
WEST PALM BEACH, Fla. — President Donald Trump on Sunday endorsed embattled Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu late in his reelection campaign, a coveted boost because of Trump's popularity but one that the American had withheld for months as the vote neared and the leaders' strategies in the Middle East diverged.
Elections in Israel will be a verdict on Netanyahu's handling of October 7
Trump said in a post on social media that Netanyahu "has been a Great Warrior Prime Minister, and an even greater Partner for Peace in combatting the Islamic Republic of Iran."
Israelis are set to vote Oct. 27, in the first national elections since Hamas attacked Israel on Oct. 7, 2023.
The absence of an endorsement was a setback for the Likud politician, who had billed his tight ties to the Republican president as a selling point for staying in power.
"Thank you, President Trump, for the tremendous support and work together," Netanyahu said in a statement in Hebrew posted on X. "Together we will complete the mission!"
His Likud Party attached to the statement a 35-second video showing clips of the two leaders together and Trump praising Netanyahu.
It was not immediately clear what impact the endorsement would have on what is expected to be a tight race. Trump is still popular in Israel, but many Israelis, including leading commentators, have expressed disappointment over the inconclusive outcome of the Iran war and Trump's ceasefire proposal, which fell short of many of the countries' key goals when they jointly launched the war in February.
Trump confirms calling Netanyahu 'crazy,' says they still get along
In his post, Trump again threatened to "soon finish the job" against Iran "unless they change their ways, rapidly," but added he might not want to if Netanyahu loses the election. "If Israel has the wrong Leader, which would be anyone other than Bibi, America will have to do this work ourselves, and I am not inclined to do so," Trump said.
He said Netanyahu had been "Strong, Brave, and Brilliant in the face of tremendous opposition, including that coming from even Israel, itself."
Trump endorsed Netanyahu in past elections and in November he sent a letter to Israeli President Isaac Herzog asking him to pardon Netanyahu on corruption charges.
But Trump's comments about Netanyahu in recent months had been much more subdued, and he repeatedly demurred when asking if he would back the prime minister. The president, when asked in a Time magazine interview published this month about backing the Israeli leader, said, "I'm not involved in politics anymore," despite his campaigning for and endorsing U.S. politicians.
Trump, especially in his second term, has sought to give his stamp of approval to candidates in foreign elections and claimed credit when they have been successful. He endorsed Hungarian Prime Minister Viktor Orbán in his unsuccessful bid for a fifth term, as well as Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi and Polish President Karol Nawrocki, and several Latin American leaders.
Trump and Netanyahu have been close allies and seemed more aligned than ever in the wake of the joint U.S.-Israeli strikes on Iran's nuclear facilities in 2025. But over the course of the nearly eight-month war with Iran this year, they have had disagreements.
Netanyahu rejects Trump's Gaza Peace Plan, demands Hamas disarm first
Trump criticized Israel's decision to attack a key Iranian gas field in the first weeks of the war. In June, he called Netanyahu "crazy." Trump also said that he was "a little bit perturbed" as Israel's fighting with Hezbollah militants in Lebanon was holding back peace talks with Iran.
Beyond Iran, Netanyahu in August rejected Trump's latest plan to advance a stalled ceasefire in Gaza, pushing back on the U.S. administration's call for Israel to withdraw from territory it controls in Gaza until Hamas has been disarmed.

European Natural Gas Prices Hold Near Four-Year Highs as Supply Risks and Borrowing Costs Remain in Focus
Gas tap ©Adobe Stock Images European wholesale natural gas prices were broadly unchanged on Monday, remaining near their highest levels since 2022 as investors assessed supply constraints linked to the Middle East and the potential effects of higher borrowing costs on energy demand.
The benchmark Dutch Title Transfer Facility (TTF) front-month futures contract traded at approximately €87.88 per megawatt-hour (MWh), while the equivalent UK National Balancing Point (NBP) contract stood at around 217.20 pence per therm.
Despite limited movement during the session, European and British natural gas prices have recorded substantial increases since the beginning of 2026.
Market participants were also assessing comments from European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde concerning the impact of tighter financial conditions on households and businesses facing higher energy costs.
Dutch TTF natural gas futures traded at approximately €87.88/MWh on Monday, remaining close to levels last recorded in 2022.
The European benchmark has increased by approximately 210% since the beginning of the year, according to the supplied market data.
In the United Kingdom, NBP natural gas futures were broadly stable at 217.20 pence per therm.
The British benchmark has risen by approximately 196% year to date.
The limited price movements during Monday's session followed several months of increases in European wholesale gas prices.
Traders continued to assess the balance between constrained international supplies and the potential effects of slower economic activity on gas consumption.
Lagarde Highlights Impact of Higher Energy and Borrowing Costs
European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde addressed the economic implications of rising sovereign bond yields in remarks delivered on Saturday.
Lagarde said the recent increase in government borrowing costs was tightening financial conditions across the eurozone, raising financing expenses for households and businesses.
She indicated that these developments could reduce economic growth while making it more difficult for consumers and industrial companies to manage higher energy bills.
The increase in fuel and electricity costs has been linked in part to supply disruptions associated with continuing conflicts in the Middle East.
According to the supplied report, Lagarde suggested that the combined effect of higher borrowing costs and energy prices could place greater pressure on European households and businesses than previously anticipated by the ECB.
ECB Interest Rate Expectations Add to Demand Considerations
Financial markets are anticipating another European Central Bank interest rate increase in December.
According to the supplied update, investors expect a 25-basis-point rise that would bring the benchmark policy rate to 2.75%.
Such a move would place monetary policy in territory considered restrictive to economic activity.
Higher borrowing costs can affect industrial investment and operating expenditure, including activities that rely on natural gas as an energy source or production input.
The potential impact on industrial demand remains one of the factors being assessed by participants in European gas markets.
The anticipated December rate increase remains a market expectation rather than a confirmed ECB decision.
Middle East Shipping Disruptions Affect LNG Supplies
Supply conditions remain a central consideration for European natural gas markets.
Liquefied natural gas (LNG) shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz have faced restrictions associated with maritime attacks and disruptions to shipping routes.
Some vessels have been diverted around Africa, extending journey times and affecting the availability of LNG cargoes.
These developments have constrained spot LNG deliveries to European import terminals, according to the supplied market update.
The disruptions have increased uncertainty surrounding the timing and availability of imported gas supplies.
European gas prices remain sensitive to changes in shipping conditions and the ability of suppliers to deliver contracted and spot cargoes.
European Gas Storage Levels Remain Below Seasonal Norms
European Union underground gas storage levels are reported to be below historical seasonal averages as the winter withdrawal period approaches.
Lower storage inventories can reduce the volume of gas available to meet additional demand during periods of colder weather.
This makes storage conditions an important factor in assessing European supply requirements during the winter months.
The supplied report did not specify the current percentage of EU storage capacity that is filled.
Market participants will continue to monitor storage withdrawals, LNG arrivals and weather conditions as winter demand develops.
European Gas Markets Balance Supply and Demand Factors
European gas prices remained broadly stable on Monday, with Dutch TTF futures at €87.88/MWh and UK NBP futures at approximately 217.20 pence per therm.
Both benchmarks have recorded substantial year-to-date increases, reflecting changes in global gas supply conditions.
Supply disruptions linked to the Middle East and below-average European storage inventories remain relevant to price developments.
At the same time, higher borrowing costs and expectations of further ECB monetary tightening are being assessed for their potential impact on industrial and household energy demand.

Samsung Reportedly Taps TSMC for Nvidia&#x27;s Next-Gen Memory - Taiwan Semiconductor (NYSE:TSM) - Benzinga
SPY — QQQ 707.87 −0.27% BTC/USD — DIA — GLD — TLT 80.06 +0.01%
Samsung Turns to Rival TSMC for Nvidia’s Next-Generation Memory—Micron, SK Hynix Face Same Request: Report
Samsung Electronics Co. Ltd. (OTC: SSNLF ) will reportedly use Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. Ltd. ’s (NYSE: TSM ) process to make the base die for Nvidia Corp .’s (NASDAQ: NVDA ) custom high-bandwidth memory.
In August, Nvidia unveiled NVHBM, a custom design that integrates the memory controller into the base die and can deliver up to 30% more memory bandwidth and 15% lower power consumption than standard HBM4E.
The AI bellwether has asked Samsung, SK Hynix Inc. (NASDAQ: SKHY ), and Micron Technology Inc. (NASDAQ: MU ), the three suppliers tapped to produce NVHBM, to manufacture the base die, the logic layer at the bottom of the HBM stack, on TSMC’s 3-nanometer process, industry sources told the Seoul Economic Daily.
It also wants the core die to be standardized on the 6th-generation 1c DRAM process.
Samsung’s Device Solutions division accepted the request after an internal review, the report added.
Samsung, SK Hynix, Micron and Nvidia did not immediately respond to Benzinga’s request for comment.
TSMC declined to comment on the report, saying it does not comment on individual customers.
In a 2024 statement on memory collaboration, the company said it has worked with partners including Micron, Samsung Memory, and SK Hynix on high-bandwidth memory solutions aiming to “tackle the rapidly growing demand in AI.”
Will S&P 500 open higher? Polymarket odds reach 88% as Wall Street shakes off &#34;Black Monday&#34; crash fears ahead of Meta and Tesla earnings.
Samsung worked with TSMC four years ago on packaging compatibility, but this is the first time the two have partnered at the HBM manufacturing stage, the report said.
Samsung will maintain its turnkey model for standard HBM and custom HBM supplied to other customers.
Chief Market Strategist at Futurum Equities Shay Boloor sees the setup giving Nvidia “more flexibility to shift orders between suppliers as HBM prices rise,” while TSMC stands to benefit “regardless of which memory maker wins the business.”
JPMorgan’s analysis shows HBM shortages of 20% in 2026, 19% in 2027, and 16% in 2028, with the cumulative shortage reaching 23 weeks by 2028.
Memory chip makers are also facing intensified competition with China , as memory maker ChangXin Memory Technologies reported progress on a denser DRAM design and plans server-memory products using it by the end of 2026.
Price Action: NYSE-listed shares of TSMC closed 2.28% lower on Friday at $412.6 and fell 0.04% in after-hours trading. In Taiwan, the stock was trading flat at TWD 2,420 ($74.53) on Monday.

### 42. ★★★☆☆ France braces for weeks more of protests as unrest spreads to Italy – Europe live

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T09:37:47+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Gilets jaunes movement calls for further demonstrations in France as Giorgia Meloni schedules crisis talks after looting in Italian citiesMacron criticises ‘unbridled violence’ after 2,400 arrests across Francein RomeItalian prime minister Giorgia Meloni will hold a crisis meeting with top security officials on Monday

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

France braces for weeks more of protests as unrest spreads to Italy – Europe live
Gilets jaunes movement calls for further demonstrations in France as Giorgia Meloni schedules crisis talks after looting in Italian cities
Macron criticises ‘unbridled violence’ after 2,400 arrests across France
Mon 19 Oct 2026 06.20 EDT First published on Mon 19 Oct 2026 03.44 EDT
Italy&#x27;s Meloni to hold crisis talks after clashes with police in Milan, Turin, Bologna
Protesters block Louvre entrances in Paris as they back student campaign
Marseille bans public gatherings, demonstrations today
Zelenskyy confirms no intelligence on overnight attacks, says US &#x27;in denial&#x27; about Russia&#x27;s position
Police clashes with protesters as protests spread to Italy, as fuel, fresh food shortages also hit south
Morning opening: Macron declines offer of interior minister&#x27;s resignation after weekend riots
A fire is seen with the Eiffel Tower in the background during clashes on Saturday Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images
A fire is seen with the Eiffel Tower in the background during clashes on Saturday Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images
There are no signs of French protests stopping anytime soon: the yellow vest movement, de gilets jaunes , has just announced a “second act” of protests for upcoming Saturday, 24 October – and then again a week after that, too.
The idea is to “keep the momentum going” until another major demonstration planned for 5 November, a group member told AFP.
A car burns near a graffiti reading "introduction to civil war" as clashes erupted during a demonstration against the "high cost of living", in Paris last weekend. Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images
Italy&#x27;s Meloni to hold crisis talks after clashes with police in Milan, Turin, Bologna
Protesters block Louvre entrances in Paris as they back student campaign
Marseille bans public gatherings, demonstrations today
Zelenskyy confirms no intelligence on overnight attacks, says US &#x27;in denial&#x27; about Russia&#x27;s position
Police clashes with protesters as protests spread to Italy, as fuel, fresh food shortages also hit south
Morning opening: Macron declines offer of interior minister&#x27;s resignation after weekend riots
We are expecting to hear from France’s prime minister Sébastien Lecornu later today, but he is currently chairing an emergency meeting on the events of the weekend.
France's interior Minister Laurent Nuñez and prime minister Sebastien Lecornu attend an interministerial crisis meeting in Paris, France. Photograph: Julien de Rosa/EPA Party leaders will also meet with the PM to discuss the political fallout from the protests.
Curiously, the far-right National Rally party will not be represented neither by its presidential candidate, Marine Le Pen, nor its prime ministerial candidate, Jordan Bardella, but a more junior figure, media reported.
In the meantime, Paris prosecutor said at least 145 people were still in custody following “various acts of vandalism, looting and attacks on law enforcement,” as reported by Le Figaro.
A person wears a mask near burning bicycles during civil unrest, the day after a national day of demonstrations against the cost of living and rising fuel prices, in Paris, France. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters in Rome
Italian prime minister Giorgia Meloni will hold a crisis meeting with top security officials on Monday after two days of ransacking and looting in Milan allegedly perpetrated by gangs of hooded youths.
The unrest began on Saturday on the fringes of a protest against the rising cost of living. There were also clashes between police and protesters in Turin and Bologna.
Clashes in Bologna last weekend. Photograph: Alessandro Ruggeri/LaPresse/Shutterstock In Milan, more than 200 were detained by police, allegedly responsible for ransacking shops and smashing windows, including in the area surrounding the city’s Duomo. According to reports in the Italian press on Monday, 80% were minors, with many responding to appointments made on social media and sharing selfies as they bragged about the damage caused.
They were not believed to have been affiliated with any political party or organised groups or movements.
Meloni said on Saturday that the perpetrators were “thugs who exploit demonstrations to spread chaos, attack those who guarantee security, and hold entire cities to hostage”.
“To demonstrate is a right. To lay our cities to waste is not,” she wrote on X.
Police patrol streets after clashes with youth protesters near Piazza del Duomo in Milan, Italy. Photograph: Alessandro Bremec/NurPhoto/Shutterstock During her more than four years in power, Meloni has enacted tough security measures, including against peaceful protesters.
In July, her cabinet approved so-called “anti-maranza” legislation in response to a rise in child gangs and youth delinquency in city centres. “Maranza” is a slang term used to describe gangs of delinquent youths, usually coming from suburban areas.
Opposition politicians often accuse the government of using security as a propaganda tool, arguing that Italy’s leaders do little to address core issues such as education, housing and living conditions in marginalised areas.
There are no signs of French protests stopping anytime soon: the yellow vest movement, de gilets jaunes , has just announced a “second act” of protests for upcoming Saturday, 24 October – and then again a week after that, too.
The idea is to “keep the momentum going” until another major demonstration planned for 5 November, a group member told AFP.
A car burns near a graffiti reading "introduction to civil war" as clashes erupted during a demonstration against the "high cost of living", in Paris last weekend. Photograph: Guillaume Saligot/AFP/Getty Images Protesters blocked entrances to the Louvre museum in Paris in support of a nationwide high school student protest movement and to denounce “police repression”, the museum’s management and unions told AFP.
The museum could not open as scheduled because of a protest led by students from the Ecole du Louvre, management said. A leaflet displayed outside the museum read: “We are proud to announce that we are blocking the world’s largest museum.”
Protesters and students from art and culture schools block the entrance to the Louvre in Paris, France. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters Back to France , the authorities in Marseille have just issued a ban of any public gatherings and demonstrations “throughout the entire commune” from midday to 8pm.
Violent clashes and damage occurred before the start of a protest on the Canebiere and at the Old Port against the high cost of living in Marseille, France. Photograph: Alain ROBERT/SIPA/Shutterstock Meanwhile, Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy has responded to the latest wave of Russian attacks on Ukraine, confirming Ukraine had no prior warning from the US and seemingly accusing Washington of being in the state of denial about the reality of the war.
A woman stands next to a damaged beauty store near a metro station, which was hit by an overnight Russian missile strike, amid Russia's attack on Ukraine, in Kyiv, Ukraine. Photograph: Valentyn Ogirenko/Reuters “Another Russian ballistic missile attack came in several waves overnight, and once again, there was no early warning from our partners, ” he said.
“They again targeted energy facilities and infrastructure, but a medical clinic, a school, a metro station, and ordinary residential buildings were also damaged.”
Showing growing frustration with the attacks and continued criticism from US president Donald Trump , he said:
“ Ukraine never wanted this war, and we have welcomed literally EVERY attempt by our partners to bring this war to a dignified end. We have responded positively to EVERY ceasefire proposal. Russia’s response, time and again, has been ballistic missiles, “shaheds,” and killings.
Putin not only doesn’t want to end this war – he doesn’t even want to take any steps toward de-escalation. EVERY ONE of our partners has experienced this firsthand when they heard Russia’s refusals.
And it is crucial to call things what they are. To call the aggressor an aggressor and help Ukraine defend lives. When deliveries of missiles for Ukraine’s air defence are blocked while Russia is granted relief or delays in sanctions, this is, unfortunately, a denial of reality on the part of our partners.
The reality is that the war continues, and it continues solely because of Russian strikes and Russia’s desire to drag this war out.”
Italian police also clashed with protesters who threw stones and looted shops in Milan on Sunday during demonstrations over the rising cost of living, as lorry drivers blocked roads across southern Italy in protest at soaring fuel prices linked to the war in Iran.
Demonstrators light flares during an anti-government protest organised by the Italia in Piazza movement, at Piazza Duomo in Milan. Photograph: Piero Cruciatti/AFP/Getty Images Footage showed police in riot gear firing teargas and using water cannon after several hundred hooded protesters smashed shop windows and set bins alight in the area around Piazza del Duomo in central Milan.
Second day of the demonstration "Italia in piazza" against the high cost of living in Milan, Italy. Photograph: Nicola Marfisi/AGF/Shutterstock View image in fullscreen
Second day of the demonstration “Italia in piazza” against the high cost of living in Milan, Italy. Photograph: Alessandro Bremec/NurPhoto/Shutterstock The clashes echoed scenes of violence in neighbouring France , where hundreds of thousands of people angered by rising living costs and declining public services took to the streets on Saturday in some of the country’s most violent protests in years.
Meanwhile, lorry drivers have caused widespread disruption across southern Italy, blocking major roads and creating huge traffic jams in Sicily, Puglia and Calabria in protest at the mounting financial pressure on the haulage industry.
A handwritten sign reading ''Tutto esaurito'' (All sold out) covers the diesel and petrol price board at a self-service petrol station in Barletta, Italy. Photograph: Matteo Della Torre/NurPhoto/Shutterstock Hundreds of petrol stations have run out of fuel, while supermarkets are beginning to report shortages of several products, particularly fresh food. Pharmacies have also been affected, raising concerns about disruption to essential supplies.
“We are asking the government to urgently convene a meeting with industry associations. Otherwise, we will consider a nationwide strike,” said Pasquale Russo, president of Conftrasporto, an association representing Italian haulage companies.
“The situation is very concerning because the excessive price increases affecting the haulage industry have created a highly tense situation among transport companies, leading to spontaneous protests in some areas of the country,” he added.
In several parts of southern Italy, petrol stations have run out of both petrol and diesel, with motorists facing long queues at the few filling stations still operating. The road blockades have also caused extensive traffic disruption, with fears growing that prolonged protests could further disrupt supplies of food, fuel and other essential goods.
Fuel pumps are cordoned off with red and white barrier tape at a Q8 Easy self-service station in Barletta, Italy. Photograph: Matteo Della Torre/NurPhoto/Shutterstock French president Emmanuel Macron has declined interior minister Laurent Nuñez’s offer to resign after widespread riots over the weekend, despite “failures in anticipation and intelligence” that were deemed to worsen the crisis engulfing the country.
Macron said Nuñez should stay to ‌ensure a chain of command is retained within the police and security forces, and has also asked Nuñez to set up ‌as quickly as possible new approaches to deal with looters, ​the Élysée Palace added, quoted by Reuters.
Macron has asked the minister to “put in place new operational methods for security forces” to get a grip over the crisis.
Saturday, one of the most violent days of unrest in years, left public buildings burned, hundreds of shops destroyed and looted and at least 2,400 people arrested in cities across the country .
In a TV interview on Monday, Nuñez admitted that the police “lost control” in some cities during the protests.
Riots in central Paris over the weekend, with 2,400 people arrested nationally. Photograph: Poitout Florian/ABACA/Shutterstock In an address to the nation last night, Macron criticised “unbridled violence”.
Emmanuel Macron condemns violence in televised address – video
“Watching these images, we have all asked ourselves how could French people do this to France ? ” he said.
Macron criticises ‘unbridled violence’ after days of unrest and 2,400 arrests across France
Separately, I will also be keeping an eye on Ukraine , after another night of heavy attacks by Russia killing at least one and injuring eight people, and with conflicting reports about whether Kyiv authorities had been warned about the strikes.
A police officer inspects the site of a fitness club building hit by a Russian missile strike, amid Russia’s attack on Ukraine, in Kyiv, Ukraine. Photograph: Valentyn Ogirenko/Reuters We should also get an update from Sweden , as the parliament’s speaker is set to decide on the next step in the government formation process there.
It’s Monday, 19 October 2026 , it’s Jakub Krupa here, and this is Europe Live .

Natural Gas and Oil Forecast: Tanker Attacks Rattle Gulf, Can WTI Break $98.31?
Two tankers were attacked off Saudi Arabia, and Iran’s Revolutionary Guards said two more hit mines in Hormuz. Meanwhile, Trump waived Graham Act tariffs of up to 100% on top buyers of Russian energy.
WTI trades near $97.36 with a slight bullish lean above $96.81 support. A break above $98.31 could reopen $99.23 and $100.14, while a close below $96.11 would break the trendline structure.
Brent holds near $107.74 after a triangle breakout, keeping a slight bullish lean above $107.00. A move above $108.84 would confirm the breakout toward $109.97, while a close below $105.14 would invalidate it.
Natural gas trades near $3.08 with a bearish lean after breaking its rising trendline as mild weather limits demand. A close below $3.03 could expose $2.97 and $2.91, while a recovery above $3.15 would invalidate the breakdown.
Oil Supply Risks Widen as Washington Eases Pressure on Russia
Crude oil markets went in two different directions with regards to oil supply and fundamentals. Policy changes in Washington may ease restrictions on supply. Attacks on oil supply and other critical infrastructure constrain the physical supply of oil.
Next up Oil News: Brent Pulls Back From $110 as Weekend Threats Fail to Extend Rally
President Trump invoked the Graham Act ’s national interest waiver on Friday, suspending the imposition of Section 112 and 113 duties on Russian energy and other products two days before the deadline. Among the suspended provisions are levies of up to 100% on the largest buyers of Russian crude and other energy products. Executive Orders issued before the Act are the basis for the sanctions on Russia that remain in place. A temporary ease on sanctions on the export of Russian diesel was reported earlier this month.
US drilling activity held steady this week. The Baker Hughes rig count was unchanged at 603 as of October 16, with oil rig counts down 3 at 459 and gas up 1 at 133.
The UK Maritime Trade Operations Centre said two tankers were attacked off Saudi Arabia in the Persian Gulf on Friday, with one hit by a rocket-propelled grenade fired from a small boat. Meanwhile, Iran’s Revolutionary Guards said two other tankers, each carrying about 2 million barrels of crude, caught on fire after hitting naval mines in the Strait of Hormuz. The Saudi-led coalition said it intercepted several missiles over the Red Sea on Friday, including one aimed at the oil export hub of Yanbu. Additionally, Ukraine also struck an oil terminal in St. Petersburg on Sunday.
Lower 48 production has averaged 110.8 bcfd in October so far, below the August and September records of 113.3 bcfd, due to disruptions and issues with pipelines and the Gulf of Mexico. Mild weather is currently limiting natural gas demand, and LSEG expects it to rise to 106.8 bcfd during the next two weeks from 103.87 bcfd this week. LNG feedgas was expected to reach a five-month high of 18.9 bcfd on Friday.
Every new Natural Gas analysis as it publishes, today&#x27;s technical signal and key levels, live price — on one page.
Natural Gas Technical Analysis: NG Slips Below $3.09 as $3.03 Support Comes Into Focus
Natural gas is trading at about $3.08/MMBtu. The price has broken and closed below the rising trend line that had been in place since early October, which is bearish. Further, the price has traded below both the 10- and 20-period moving averages. Furthermore, the 10-period moving average is rolling over and crossing below the 20-period moving average, which is also bearish.
Bullish, near term, I would look to defend the price of $3.03, where the latest move lower found buying. Below that, the price of $2.97 and $2.91 would be targeted. These levels correspond to the October lows. From a bullish perspective, I would look for the price to close above the $3.15 level, where sellers were defending the price after the breakdown.
RSI is currently at 37 and has flattened out at its 50-period moving average, which is neutral, but is still bearish. As long as $3.09 continues to cap the upside of natural gas, I would maintain a bearish bias and look for selling opportunities. A move below $3.03 would further strengthen the bear case while a move above $3.15 would negate the bear case.
WTI Technical Analysis: USOIL Holds $97.27 as $98.31 Comes Into Focus
WTI is trading at $97.36 on the 1-hour chart. What I see is the pullback from the high at $99.24 has slowed at the 50% Fibonacci retracement at $97.27. Below that is the $96.81 to $97.26 support zone, which is confluent with the rising trendline. As long as the moving averages are sloping up, and WTI is trading above both of them, the prevailing trend remains up.
In the near-term, the first level of resistance is at $97.74 (the 38.2% level), with the next level at $98.31. A break above that level opens $99.23 and then $100.14. The first level of support is at $96.81 (the 61.8% level) with further support at $96.11 and $95.30.
RSI is at 53 and the trend line is flat, indicating momentum has cooled rather than turned bearish. My bias is slightly bullish for the near-term as long as WTI stays above $96.81. I’d be looking for a close above $98.31 to add further evidence of a bullish continuation. A close below $96.11 would likely break the rising trendline.
Brent Technical Analysis: UKOIL Holds $107.56 as $108.84 Comes Into Focus
Brent is currently trading at $107.74, according to the 1-hour chart. I’m currently looking at the breakout from the contracting triangle, which propelled the price up to $109.97, before selling pressure set in. The pullback is currently sitting at the $107.56 50% Fibonacci retracement. The rising trendline and the fast moving average are both converging and providing support at the $107.00 area.
In the near term, the first resistance I’m looking at is at $108.13, followed by $108.84. The area between these two levels is the close-above target for the contracting triangle and was previously resistance. A clean break above this area would bring the price up to the $109.97 high. On the other hand, the $107.00 area remains key support, and the projected path of the chart suggests a possible retest of this area before a move higher. $105.14 is the next key support level further down.
RSI is at 53, and is trending down from 71, a potential bearish signal. That said, I am a bit bullish as long as Brent stays above $107.00. A close above $108.84 would confirm the bullish breakout, while a close below $105.14 would invalidate it.

South Korea Turns Back to Coal as LNG Crisis Deepens
Greenpeace Protest Forces Shutdown at Woodside’s $12.5 Billion Gas Project
Two Greenpeace activists were arrested in Australia after they caused a halt in operations at Woodside Energy's Scarborough LNG platform, off the coast of Western Australia. The two were charged with "interfering with offshore petroleum installations or operations", the local authorities said in a statement as cited by ABC. The maximum penalty for that charge is 10 years in prison.
The two activists boarded the platform on Saturday and spent the night there, Reuters reported , citing a Greenpeace statement that the activists would "occupy the platform for as long as possible" in expression of protest against a new project led by Woodside that, according to the group, would damage a coral reef system that is inhabited by an endangered species of whale. The company disagrees, saying there will be no contact between its production facilities and the coral ecosystem.
The Browse project is estimated to cost around $34 billion to develop, tapping into the largest undeveloped natural gas deposit in Australia, according to ABC.
The Scarborough LNG facility is 98% complete. With a price tag of $12.5 billion , the facility is planned to produce some 8 million tons of liquefied natural gas annually, plus 6 million cum of natural gas, to be supplied to the domestic market. Per schedule, Scarborough was supposed to go into operation before the end of this year, but the two activists' actions this last weekend may prompt a postponement, based on Woodside's comments on the events and the fact that operations at the facility remained on pause.
"Greenpeace's actions have triggered a significant, potentially costly and entirely avoidable response, requiring Western Australian resources to be mobilised to manage the safety, security and operational consequences of their actions. Contrary to Greenpeace's claimed mission, the behaviour of its representatives&hellip; has endangered the surrounding environment, in addition to people and the facility itself," a spokesperson for the energy major told Upstream.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Federal Reserve raises interest rates for the 1st time since 2023
Trump criticized the moving, calling on the Fed to "LOWER THE INTEREST RATES."
The Federal Reserve raised interest rates on Wednesday in an effort to battle back a monthslong surge of inflation set off by the Iran war . The move marks the central bank&#x27;s first rate increase since July 2023.
The central bank hiked its benchmark rate a quarter of a percentage point, putting interest rates at a level between 3.75% and 4%.
Hours after the announcement, President Donald Trump criticized the move, repeating a call for lower interest rates that he has made on multiple occasions since his return to office last year.
The Federal Reserve Building is the headquarters of the Federal Reserve System and 12 Federal Reserve Banks, in Washington, D.C.
"LOWER THE INTEREST RATES FOR THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA, AND FAST!" Trump said in a social media post.
However later, Trump told ABC News that despite the Fed doing the opposite of what he has demanded he still has confidence in Fed Chair Kevin Warsh.
"I’m relying on Kevin, but he’s got a very tough board," Trump said from the tarmac while traveling to a campaign rally in North Carolina, referring to the 12-member policymaking board. "He&#x27;s got a board that was put there by a lot of other people, and the interest rates are too high, they’re not appropriate."
Trump insisted the Fed raised rates “only for political reasons” and to hurt him.
“They’re raising the rates to make Trump do as bad as he can possibly do," he said. "The problem they have is that we have the greatest economy in history."
Earlier Wednesday, the board, including Trump-appointed Warsh, voted unanimously in favor of the rate increase.
That current level of interest rates marks a significant drop from a recent peak attained in 2023, but borrowing costs remain well above a 0% rate established at the outset of the COVID-19 pandemic.
Iran live updates: Sticker shock woes rise after Saudi refinery stops crude loading
The major stock indexes turned lower after the policy announcement. The Dow Jones Industrial Average closed down 630 points, or 1.2%, while the S & P 500 dropped 0.4%. The tech-heavy Nasdaq ticked down 0.01%.
"The plain fact is that inflation is too high and has been for too long," Warsh said at a press conference in Washington, D.C., on Wednesday. "The committee’s unanimous vote shows our resolve to achieve price stability on a timelier basis."
The move appears aimed at containing a recent bout of price increases. Global oil prices are hovering near a four-month high and the average price for a gallon of gasoline tops $4.30, according to AAA .
The U.S. economy has shown signs of additional strain in recent days, including a bond selloff that is pushing up borrowing costs for credit cards and mortgages.
The price woes previously divided central bankers eager to contain inflation but reluctant to cool off the economy and weaken the labor market.
The Fed opted to hold interest rates steady at its most recent meeting in July. Three of the 12 members on the Fed&#x27;s policymaking board, however, voted in favor of a rate hike, marking the largest number of dissenters casting ballots in the same direction in a decade.
Bend, Oregon, shooting: Suspect ID&#x27;d, had attended elementary school, police say
2 dead, 11 injured after Kansas mother drives onto soccer field during game: Police
Atlanta school worker dies after falling from Six Flags roller coaster in Georgia
Warsh, who took the helm of the central bank in May, has vowed to cool off persistently elevated price increases.
"The Fed&#x27;s predominant focus right now should be on prices," Warsh said in remarks last month at the Fed&#x27;s annual summer gathering in Jackson Hole, Wyoming.
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh speaks during a news conference following Federal Open Market Committee meetings at Federal Reserve Headquarters, September 16, 2026 in Washington.
Prices rose 3.4% in August compared to a year earlier, maintaining the same level from the prior month, federal government data last week showed.
Inflation stands more than a percentage point higher than the Federal Reserve&#x27;s target rate of 2%.
Despite a stubborn bout of inflation, the economy remains fairly robust by some measures.
A blockbuster jobs report earlier this month showed employers added 162,000 workers in August, demonstrating continued resilience for the nation&#x27;s labor market. The economy grew over three months ending in June, defying fear of a downturn triggered by the Iran war.
Fighting in the Middle East kept global crude prices above $105 a barrel as of Wednesday, which amounted to a rise of about 50% since the Iran war broke out in late February.
Inflation holds steady at elevated level as Iran war pushes up gas prices
The average price of a gallon of gas in the U.S. stands at $4.36, putting it more than $1.30 higher than before the war, AAA data showed.
Record-high diesel prices have raised transport costs for many everyday products, including groceries, clothes and furniture.
The war in Iran, which began with a large-scale U.S.-Israeli attack in the winter, prompted Iran&#x27;s near-closure of the Strait of Hormuz, which facilitates one-fifth of global crude supply. Saudi Arabia over the weekend shut down a key pipeline bypassing the strait, which further constrained oil delivery and put upward pressure on prices.
ABC News&#x27; Karen Travers, Michelle Stoddart and Devin Garbitt contributed to this report.

### 47. ★★★☆☆ Robot factories and hypersonic missiles: JPMorgan Chase’s first $5 billion of defense investments

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-19T10:00:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: With $5 billion already deployed off its balance sheet, JPMorgan Chase is racing ahead on direct investments in defense tech, hardware and supply chains.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

JPMorgan&#x27;s $5 billion bet on U.S. security; startups Castelion and Hadrian
JPMorgan Chase has deployed $5 billion into startup investments in the first year of its U.S. security and resiliency initiative, halfway to its initial $10 billion goal.
The bank may exceed that target, co-president Doug Petno told CNBC.
JPMorgan has taken stakes in startups Hadrian, which builds automated factories for aerospace and defense firms, and Castelion, which is developing low-cost hypersonic missiles.
Separately, JPMorgan has financed and facilitated more than $230 billion in transactions in the initiative’s first year, part of a broader goal to support $1.5 trillion in activity over a decade.
Todd Combs, portfolio manager at Berkshire Hathaway Inc., waits for the start of the "Berkshire Hathaway Invest In Yourself 5K" race presented by Brooks Sports, a Berkshire Hathaway Inc. company, on the sidelines of the Berkshire Hathaway shareholders meeting in Omaha, Nebraska, U.S., on Sunday, May 4, 2014.
JPMorgan Chase has made $5 billion in startup investments in the first year of its U.S. security and resiliency initiative , the company said Monday, a faster-than-expected pace that indicates the bank is finding plentiful opportunities in emerging defense, manufacturing and supply chain firms.
The bank, which last year said it would deploy $10 billion into startups over a decade, may expand the program beyond that initial dollar amount, JPMorgan co-president Doug Petno told CNBC in an interview.
Todd Combs, the former Berkshire Hathaway executive who leads the JPMorgan group in charge of making startup investments , has "hit the ground running," Petno said. "He&#x27;s done a great job and put us in some really interesting companies," he added.
Most of those investments have yet to be disclosed, but two known stakes seem emblematic of the project&#x27;s mission to bolster companies that are needed for a more dangerous and chaotic era of geopolitics. JPMorgan, the biggest U.S. bank by assets, has taken direct stakes in defense-related startups Hadrian and Castelion .
Wars in Ukraine and the Middle East, as well as trade disputes with China, have threatened the flow of energy and key components needed for U.S. industries and defense, which spurred JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon into creating the initiative a year ago.
He put Petno, widely seen as one of two key possible successors , in charge of the overall investment project. Combs, a former board member of the bank, reports directly to Dimon.
The scope of the project extends beyond supply chain, defense and manufacturing to include frontier technologies and the pharmaceutical and health-tech industries.
Co-CEOs of Commercial & Investment Bank at JPMorganChase, Troy Rohrbaugh and Douglas Petno.
"There&#x27;s a lot of really interesting companies that have been working to solve some of these problems," Petno said.
These "early venture-stage, advanced manufacturing companies have found ways to build these capabilities in the U.S." rather than relying on overseas trading partners, he said.
In August, the bank helped lead a $1.4 billion funding round in Hadrian, which builds automated factories that make parts for the defense, aerospace and maritime sectors. A California factory run by Hadrian has 10 robots for every human employee, according to the startup&#x27;s founder.
That month, JPMorgan was also involved in a $1 billion funding round for Castelion, whose flagship product is a relatively low-cost hypersonic missile called Blackbeard. Castelion was founded in late 2022 by a group of former SpaceX engineers.
"Our approach is to find generational companies and partner with them for the long term," Combs said in a statement Monday.
"Putting more than $5 billion to work in our first year shows how many exceptional businesses are building in industries that strengthen critical supply chains," Combs said.
That JPMorgan would make billions of dollars of direct investments in nascent companies is a departure from its usual role as a lender and advisor.
Before the defense initiative, the bank had moved away from taking startup stakes, which can be relatively risky bets on unproven technologies. JPMorgan spun off its private equity business, One Equity Partners, more than a decade ago.
In its more traditional role, JPMorgan has also financed and facilitated more than $230 billion for industries that support American national interests, according to the bank.
That part of its security and resiliency project, meant to hit $1.5 trillion over 10 years, is led by Jay Horine , a former co-head of investment banking.
JPMorgan built a team of about three dozen bankers for the project, who are aided by hundreds more across the firm, as it expanded the initiative to Europe and Canada.
Its bankers are mapping distribution lines for key products, from submarines and missiles to aircraft, to identify suppliers that may lack the capital or capacity to meet rising demand, according to Petno.
JPMorgan is also in regular communication with the U.S. Department of Defense and Department of Commerce about the government&#x27;s needs, he said.
Hadrian CEO Chris Power: AI powered factories can rebuild American manufacturing

### 51. ★★★☆☆ 60 Minutes Transcript: Helium in short supply as Iran war continues, with spot prices tripling - CBS News

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-18T23:39:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: 60 Minutes Transcript: Helium in short supply as Iran war continues, with spot prices tripling&nbsp;&nbsp;CBS News

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

You've no doubt noticed that since the war with Iran broke out, gas prices have spiked: up more than 50% since January. But there's another, less well known, natural resource also impacted by the conflict: helium. The world &mdash; and the war &mdash; don't function without it. No helium? No new cellphones, laptops, cars, MRI machines, missiles: all require this finite and irreplaceable resource. The closing of the Strait of Hormuz in February trapped one-third of the global helium supply. Then just weeks later: Iranian strikes on Qatar's major gas facility further rattled the supply chain. With the extent of the damage unknown, American industry is left desperate for answers as the helium crisis remains up in the air.
Last march as a new war raged, Iranian missiles and drones hit military targets throughout the Middle East: oil tankers stuck in the Persian Gulf, and energy infrastructure. All of it meant to destabilize the global economy.
One of those high value targets attacked: Ras Laffan in Qatar - the world's largest facility for natural gas &ndash; and its byproduct helium. 7,100 miles away at the University of Chicago, Joe Sachleben was watching closely from his lab.
Jon Wertheim: When you heard the news that there was an attack on the facility in Qatar, where did your thoughts go?
Joe Sachleben: Um I hope that people weren't hurt was first. And then second, it's like, this is gonna be a problem. I mean, I won't say the words - I was thinking. But, you know, it's, like, crap. We're gonna have to deal with this and I might not be able to get helium.
Helium is critical for those large cylinders: nuclear magnetic resonance machines. In Sachleben's lab, these machines are used by students, but also for higher stakes research to develop breast cancer drugs, immunotherapies, and treatments for Alzheimer's. Suddenly, it's all imperiled, collateral damage from the Iran war.
Helium is the coldest liquid on earth. So every couple months, Sachleben uses this long tube to pour 100 liters of helium on these magnets to stabilize them at 452 degrees below zero Fahrenheit.
Joe Sachleben: Once it gets to a critical level, we have to put helium back in the magnet to keep it cold.
Joe Sachleben: If that doesn't happen, w-- we break the magnet.
Jon Wertheim: You-- you've got problems if that doesn't--
Jon Wertheim: You're saying there-- there's no workaround here. There-- there's no other substance you can substitute in?
Sachleben's lab contracts with one supplier to deliver the helium he needs each month. First U of Chicago's supplier placed Sachleben and his colleagues on what's called "allocation," essentially rationing.
Joe Sachleben: They basically have said, "Well, we'll give you 50% of what you used last year." That doesn't help us. And so what we have to do is decide which machine are we going to sacrifice and which one are we gonna try to keep.
Joe Sachleben: That's where we are now if we can't get the helium.
Joe Sachleben: Fortunately, we found another supplier that is giving us helium on a fairly regular basis. But they can't guarantee it. So we never know if they're gonna say, "Oh, this week, we don't have any."
Then last month, Sachleben got a message saying that supplier was about to run out.
It's not just university research labs. One fifth of the world's helium is consumed by the semiconductor industry alone to make every modern day electronic you might use, from your iPhone to your pacemaker.
Outside Minneapolis, we visited one of those chip makers, Polar Semiconductors.
Surya Iyer: Alright so go ahead and put that on first.
We suited up alongside Surya Iyer, Polar's president and COO, to go inside what's called the "Fab", the ultra sterilized area where they make the chips.
Our barriers to entry so to speak: two masks, two booties, and two pairs of gloves.
Jon Wertheim: You wonderhow-- how many people going to the Mall of Americas have no idea what's going on a few hundred yards away.
Inside, workers in round-the-clock shifts manufacture semiconductors for engine systems in cars, the U.S. military, and those ubiquitous data centers around the country.
Surya Iyer: I would say almost 40%, 50% of the machines in this factory would have helium in some way or the other in the machine.
Jon Wertheim: So it's not just one step of the process.
Surya Iyer: No, no, no. Hundreds of steps in the process involve helium.
Such as this machine, where helium keeps the environment clean and stable to avoid defects on the wafers.
Polar is able to hedge its helium reserves by relying on three suppliers. Still, the company has felt the effects of the shortage. One of their suppliers has put them on allocation as well - that rationing program.
Jon Wertheim: Just to be clear, if you did not have helium, what would happen in this facility?
Surya Iyer: Well, that's a situation that I don't want to even imagine,because if we do not have helium, we'll have to shut down. And&ndash;
And the helium crisis is hitting at a time when Polar Semiconductors plans on doubling production capacity to 140 million chips a month.
Surya Iyer: Demand is not going down. The AI data centers are growing lot of demand for semiconductors. Electrification is growing demand for semiconductors. And that means the demand for helium is gonna be growing for a long time.
Jon Wertheim: These data centers we keep hearing about, trillions of dollars popping up all over the U.S. Those don't happen if we don't have helium.
Surya Iyer: Oh, yeah. Without helium, none of these things happen. That's why local supply, supply in the United States is so important to us.
Besides Qatar, the United States is the other major producer of helium for the world. And most of that comes from a single facility in Wyoming. U.S. helium exports are up 70% since the war began and Ras Laffan was hit. This is mostly to accommodate countries manufacturing semiconductors. But the U.S. can't offset the shortage indefinitely.
And here's another issue: ramping up production of helium is not easy, even if you can find it. Four hours north of the Twin Cities we visited the site of a helium discovery, the Topaz Project. Cliff Cain is president of the startup exploration company.
Cliff Cain: So this is a wellhead. We drill down around 3,500 feet. We hit the pocket of gas down there, and -- up comes the gas. It will come out this gauge here. And-- that's how it goes.
And once it goes, this finite resource is gone forever.
For the record, Cain estimates about a dollar of helium is released every second that valve is open. His company has inserted seven wells around a 100-square-mile plot of land.
In most cases &mdash; including the Qatar facility &mdash; helium is a byproduct of the money-maker: natural gas extraction. In Minnesota, though, it's all helium, all the time. But finding helium is one thing. Getting it to market, even in the best case scenario, will take years.
Jon Wertheim: This is all encouraging. But this isn't gonna help us next month.
Cliff Cain: It's not gonna help us next month. It's not easy to find. It's not easy to build. The logistics are hard, right? You've gotta keep-- something that's 452 degrees below zero cold. And it's perishable.
That's because helium starts to boil off as soon as it's in transit. It's that complicated supply chain that worries Cain, making helium, he says, particularly vulnerable to disruption.
Cliff Cain: We've been trying to warn the markets about this for decades. We're too concentrated with our supply in the world. When you look at it, you've got supply in Qatar. You have some supply in Algeria. You have a little in Poland. You have a source in Russia. And we have a few here in the United States. So all it takes is a few of those to fall and the whole supply chain comes unraveled.
The longer the war with Iran continues, the tighter the global supply of helium gets. But how tight? That's difficult to answer. If helium is hard to acquire, so is information about it.
Rich Brooke: Oh, yes. Absolutely. It's a very tight-knit-- business with these six main players who are-- responsible for the majority of the sales.
Rich Brooke would know. He's a former executive at Air Liquide, one of the six major international helium suppliers, and is now an industry consultant.
Jon Wertheim: How much have prices gone up this year?
Rich Brooke: The spot market prices have tripled. And-- and they can go even higher because--
Jon Wertheim: --expensive to fill your car up with gas.
Jon Wertheim: The U.S. produces helium as well. Are there countries that had been relying on Qatar that are now coming to the U.S. for their supply?
Rich Brooke: Yes. Absolutely. Especially the countries that were-- heavily-- requiring helium for their semiconductor industries, there's only been traditionally two qualified sources. Qatar and Exxon, which is here in the U.S. When Qatar went down, those companies, which are predominantly based in places like Korea and Taiwan and Singapore, immediately started looking-- towards the U.S. for supplies.
Jon Wertheim: How long can the U.S. accommodate this demand from other countries?
Rich Brooke: Well, it depends on how long the shortage lasts. And also it depends on-- on how much product we can continue to recover out of the-- out of the caverns.
He's referring to helium storage caverns &mdash; two in the U.S., one in Germany &mdash; illustrated in this diagram in blue. They are underground, made of salt, and naturally impermeable, which makes them ideal for holding excess helium when the market is long.
Jon Wertheim: Do we have any idea how much helium is in these caverns?
Rich Brooke: So that's a closely held secret by the-- owners of the cavern. The market at one point last year was long. So they were actually taking helium and pumping it into those caverns. Of course, now they've reversed the direction.
Jon Wertheim: Three months from now, what does supply look like?
Rich Brooke: Well, if you can tell me what's gonna happen in the Strait of Hormuz between now and three months from now, I can tell you exactly what'll be happening in the helium business. Unfortunately, you can't and so I can't.
In the aftermath of the Iranian strikes on Ras Laffan in March, the Qataris said it would take at least three years to get the facility back in full operation. We wanted to know about the extent of the damage of the helium infrastructure specifically, but our requests for comment went unanswered.
With so much at stake &mdash; and so much swirling uncertainty about the state of this vital element &mdash; we enlisted Joe Bermudez to assess how bad it could get. A senior imagery analyst at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, Bermudez looked for clues using what little information we do have. It comes from a satellite&hellip;
Joe Bermudez: It'd be nice if the Qatari government-- shared a little more&hellip;
We provided Bermudez two images of an area of Ras Laffan that facilitates helium production - one before the Iranian strikes in March; one four days after.
He showed us this area where he says that it looks like water or firefighting foam dried to make a black stain on the cement.
Joe Bermudez: It looks like-- something necessitated the pipes in this area, the equipment in this area to be hosed down.
Jon Wertheim: So you're not prepared to say what it was or the severity, but something happened?
For all the unanswered questions about the extent of the damage at the Qatar facility, this we know: the helium supply chain issues are sufficiently serious that Cliff Cain has briefed policymakers in Washington.
Jon Wertheim: When you addressed the Pentagon about helium, specifically, what were you telling them?
Cliff Cain: That we have a problem. We have a single, critical point of failure in the system. We have Qatar go down. Now that single, critical point of failure is coming closer to term. And I think that's what's got us concerned, it's 'cause that's all it does take.
Cain says it's especially concerning given that on account of the war with Iran, the U.S. is experiencing a munitions shortage &mdash; and yet the Pentagon has asked some defense contractors to quadruple their production capacities of certain weapons.
Cliff Cain: You can't make modern warfare without helium. And that's just a fact. You've heard about the missiles, the hypersonics. Without helium, you can't make the fiber optics. You can't produce the munitions. You can't produce the electronics that goes on these things. And so if you don't have helium, your opportunity to make war becomes even more difficult.
We are seven months into the war now, with no end in sight. Since September, nearly two dozen ships have been hit while attempting to pass through the Strait of Hormuz. And no ships carrying helium are leaving Qatar.
Produced by Lucy Hatcher. Associate producer, Jessica Kegu. Broadcast associate, Mimi Lamarre. News associate, Eric Frank. Edited by Michael Mongulla.
